Tested against real subprocesses: a real `node` invocation, an argument containing a shell metacharacter proven to arrive as one literal argv element rather than being interpreted, a real cwd, a real timeout kill, and a nonexistent executable resolving rather than throwing. `scripts/run-analyst-live-check.ts` (`npm run agent:live-check`) seeds two evidence snapshots through the real `createHorusStore` write path, builds a task with `buildAnalystTask`, wires the real evidence tool with `createEvidenceToolWiring`, runs it with `nodeSpawn` and a real isolated working directory, and finally checks the result with `parseAnalystOutput` — the same acceptance function HORUS itself would apply.
- Consequences: `nodeSpawn` is now the only path anything in this codebase should use to launch Claude Code; nothing currently calls it outside the live-check script, since the agent boundary is still not wired into any IPC handler. The live-check script has not been run — that is the immediate next step, and it is the first true end-to-end rehearsal of the analyst task, closer to what step 4's shadow-mode replay will need than any prior verification in this batch.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-061 — The kickoff prompt names the actual evidence snapshot ids, not just a count

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — fixed, unit-tested, and reran live. `npm run agent:live-check` produced three evidence-linked observations, one review proposal, and five items correctly reported as missing rather than fabricated — all citing the real seeded snapshot ids.
- Context: the first full run of `npm run agent:live-check` — the real analyst task, the real evidence tool, the real spawn, all together for the first time (DEC-060) — passed `parseAnalystOutput` but its content showed a real failure. `buildKickoffPrompt` said "analyze the 2 referenced evidence snapshot(s)" without ever naming which two. The run record shows Claude Code guessed ids shaped like `live-check-1786138472999-1` and `-2`, found neither in the evidence store, and correctly reported it could not proceed. The schema accepted the report — `missingInformation` is exactly where an honest "I don't have what I need" belongs — but the run could never have produced an analysis, because the one thing the prompt claimed to reference was never actually said.
- Options considered: leave it, since the failure was reported honestly rather than fabricated; put the evidence ids in `--system-prompt` instead of the kickoff; put them in the kickoff, where the per-task specifics belong.
- Decision: `buildKickoffPrompt` now lists `task.evidence`'s snapshot ids explicitly. The system prompt still carries only the role's fixed rules, which apply to every task; the kickoff carries what's specific to this one, which is what a kickoff is for.
- Consequences: this is the second time in one session that running the code, not reading it, found what reading could not — DEC-058 was a permissions gap, this is a missing fact the agent needed and was never given. Both were "correct on paper," reviewed by parseAnalystOutput's own logic, and wrong regardless.

  The rerun is what actually closes this, and it did: given the corrected kickoff, Claude Code called `read_evidence_snapshot` against both real ids, reported the listing's rating and review count as observed facts (not as a score field — `assertNoScoreClaims` never triggered), reported PageSpeed's 41/11.2s performance figures the same way, correctly marked category, location, and contact information as `insufficient_data` rather than guessing or asserting their absence, and proposed the candidate for review with a rationale citing both evidence ids. This is the first fully live, end-to-end confirmation of AGENT_ARCHITECTURE step 3 — real task, real tool, real spawn, real isolation, schema-constrained output, HORUS's own acceptance check — all operating together against evidence the analyst had never seen before.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-062 — Real Finescape and SEASONS EATS evidence exists and is readable, but not from where the agent runs

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — verified live against the real file. The operator ran `claude -p` from `/tmp/horus-finescape-check` — deliberately unrelated to the repository — pointed at the actual `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite` with `HORUS_EVIDENCE_BASE` set to the repository root. Claude Code called `read_evidence_snapshot` for the real `raw_8ecb903a...` row and reported "Position 11: Finescape and Sons," matching the raw JSON exactly.
- Context: before attempting step 4, checked whether the retained Finescape and Sons and SEASONS EATS evidence the roadmap names actually exists anywhere `read_evidence_snapshot` could reach. It does: `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite`, a database separate from the one the Electron app writes, holds 64 rows across `serpapi.google_maps`, `serpapi.google_maps_reviews`, `pagespeed.mobile`, and several `google.maps.public-*` and `horus.website-analysis` sources, spanning 2026-08-06 22:59:58Z to 2026-08-07 02:37:55Z. Its schema predates DEC-047 (`payload_hash ... UNIQUE`), which does not affect reading. Its `raw_snapshots.storage_path` values are relative — `cache/phase5/raw/...` in some rows, `../../cache/phase5/raw/...` in others, both resolving correctly only from the HORUS repository root — because whatever produced this database (not the current `electron/persistence.ts` write path, which has always written absolute paths) ran from two different working directories at different times. `evidence-mcp-server.ts` runs from the isolated working directory DEC-057 deliberately created, which is never the repository root, so reading this evidence as-is would fail with a missing-file error the moment step 4 tried it.
- Options considered: leave the legacy paths as-is and require whoever runs a replay to `cd` to the repository root first (fragile, undocumented, and breaks the isolation DEC-057 exists for); rewrite `storage_path` in the legacy database to be absolute (violates the rule that raw evidence is never edited); resolve a relative `storage_path` against a caller-supplied base directory, defaulting to the reading process's own cwd for backward compatibility.
- Decision: `openReadOnlyEvidenceStore` takes an optional `basePath`, consulted only when a row's `storage_path` is relative. Every snapshot HORUS's own write path has ever produced uses an absolute path, so this changes nothing for current and future evidence. `createEvidenceToolWiring` and `evidence-mcp-server.ts` (via `HORUS_EVIDENCE_BASE`) carry it through. A shadow-mode replay against `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite` must supply the HORUS repository root as `basePath`.
- Consequences: proven twice — first with a unit test reproducing the real shape, then live against the actual `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite`, reading the real Finescape and Sons listing from a directory sharing nothing with where the evidence was originally retrieved. The first attempt at the live check hit `max_turns` before producing an answer (a test-command budget issue — 52KB of real listing data needs more turns than the earlier hand-seeded 40-byte payload did, not a code defect); raising `--max-turns` from 3 to 10 resolved it. This is the last piece that stood between here and an actual step 4 shadow-mode replay — the evidence exists, is real, and is now reachable from an isolated agent run.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-063 — DEC-062's basePath claim was wrong for SEASONS EATS; its listing row is unreachable

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — confirmed by the step 4 replay itself: the Finescape run used the repository-root basePath without error, and the SEASONS EATS run used the apps/operator basePath without error, exactly as this decision specifies.
- Context: before running the step 4 shadow replay, checked exactly which `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite` rows belong to Finescape and Sons and to SEASONS EATS, by grepping every stored payload for both names rather than trusting file names or assuming DEC-062's fix was sufficient. Finescape resolves cleanly: 3 rows (one `serpapi.google_maps` listing, two `serpapi.google_maps_reviews` pages), all with real ids, all `storage_path` values in the `cache/phase5/raw/...` (repo-root-relative) convention DEC-062 verified. SEASONS EATS does not resolve cleanly. Two problems, both found by direct inspection rather than assumption:
  1. The database mixes not two but **three** `storage_path` conventions: absolute paths (`electron/persistence.ts`'s own writes), `cache/phase5/raw/...` relative to the repository root (Finescape's rows), and `../../cache/phase5/raw/...` relative to `apps/operator` (SEASONS EATS's reachable rows). DEC-062 stated both relative conventions "resolv[e] correctly only from the HORUS repository root" — this is incorrect for the third convention. Resolving `../../cache/phase5/raw/...` against the repository root computes a path two directories above the repository (`/Users/javier/cache/phase5/...`), which does not exist; it only resolves correctly against `apps/operator` as the base. Confirmed both ways: by computing the resolved path directly, and by checking the target file exists at the location the `apps/operator`-relative resolution predicts. DEC-062's live verification tested only the Finescape row, so this gap was never exercised.
  2. 10 of the 64 rows in this database have a **NULL `id`** — including SEASONS EATS's own discovery/listing row (name, address, category, and the source rating/review count the operator's 73.06 lower bound was computed from). `read_evidence_snapshot` looks up a snapshot by `id`; a row with no id can never be returned by it, regardless of `basePath`. Only 2 near-duplicate paginated review-page snapshots for SEASONS EATS carry a real id (`raw_98a27e58...`, `raw_fa21eb48...`), both reporting the same rating (4.7) and review count (292). The other 8 NULL-id rows belong to five different restaurants (Sunshine Cuisine, Caribbean Bakery & Mini Mart, Mami's Latin Cuisines, RIZZ Lounge & Grill, and a raw search-results dump) — none of them SEASONS EATS.
- Options considered: treat DEC-062 as sufficient and let the SEASONS EATS replay fail or silently use the wrong evidence (rejected — charter 9.6/10.4: a retrieval failure is not evidence of anything, and a wrong `basePath` would make correct evidence unreachable rather than showing an honest gap); backfill an `id` onto the NULL-id rows in the legacy database so they become reachable (rejected — this is not `electron/persistence.ts`'s data and editing raw evidence to make it more convenient to read violates the same immutability rule DEC-047 exists to protect, applied retroactively to a file this project didn't produce); accept the gap, use a per-case `evidenceBasePath`, and run the SEASONS EATS replay explicitly as partial (chosen).
- Decision: per DECISIONS.md's own rule that accepted decisions are not edited to hide the past, DEC-062 is left as written — corrected here, not there. `scripts/run-shadow-replay.ts` takes a per-case `evidenceBasePath` (the repository root for Finescape, `apps/operator` for SEASONS EATS) rather than a single value for both. SEASONS EATS's replay is built from only the 2 reachable review-page snapshots and is labelled a partial replay in its own output; it cannot reproduce the evidence base the original 73.06 score was computed from, only show whether the analyst's observations from the reachable subset are consistent with the retained record.
- Consequences: the Finescape replay in step 4 can proceed as a full, evidence-complete run. The SEASONS EATS replay can only be a partial one, and any comparison against its historical decision must be read with that gap stated alongside it, not silently. This is itself an example of the finding DEC-047/DEC-062 already pointed at: `cache/phase5/horus.sqlite` was produced outside `electron/persistence.ts`'s write path and does not fully honor the conventions that path now enforces (absolute `storage_path`, non-null `id`). No further legacy-database repair is in scope for Phase 6; this decision records the gap rather than closing it.
- Supersedes: not applicable — corrects a factual claim in DEC-062 without altering DEC-062's own text.

### DEC-064 — Step 4 shadow replay run live against both retained cases; the boundary held

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — both replays reviewed and approved as recorded above.
- Context: `scripts/run-shadow-replay.ts` was run live, on the operator's own machine, against the real evidence identified in DEC-063 — `npm run build:electron`, then `npx tsx scripts/run-shadow-replay.ts finescape`, then `... seasons`. Both runs completed (`turnsUsed` 7 and 5; cost $0.4466 and $0.1075) and both outputs passed `parseAnalystOutput`. This is AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md step 4.
- Findings, Finescape and Sons (full evidence — all 3 rows reachable): the analyst reported the Maps listing's 4.7 rating across 30 reviews, correctly noted the listing record has no `website` field (unlike sibling listings in the same search), read 18 reviews across both pages as overwhelmingly positive on craftsmanship and responsiveness, surfaced the one 2-star review's "difficult personality" complaint as a judgment-relevant signal rather than an auto-reject condition, and proposed the candidate for review on a "strong reputation, weak web presence" rationale. It also flagged low review volume, no confirmed pricing/licensing/years-in-business, and no review-velocity trend as `insufficient_data`. **This qualitative read does not match the retained historical outcome** — the operator's real run retired Finescape at 48.1/100, below the 70-point threshold, using `reputation-scoring-v1`. That is not a contradiction: the analyst has no access to the scoring model (rule 3 forbids it from computing one) and was working from 3 snapshots, not the full retrieval `reputation-scoring-v1` runs against. The gap between "this reads well on 3 snapshots" and "this scored 48.1/100 on the full deterministic model" is precisely why DEC-050 keeps `reputation-scoring-v1` — unimplemented in this codebase — as the authority no agent output can substitute for. The replay demonstrates the boundary working as designed: the analyst's proposal changed no state and authorized nothing: the operator's real, lower, deterministic score is what actually kept Finescape out of outreach, not the analyst's tentative read of a review sample.
- Findings, SEASONS EATS (partial evidence — 2 of an unknown larger set, per DEC-063): the analyst reported the 4.7/292 rating consistent with the retained record, noted the second snapshot is a paginated superset of the first (same `dataId`, 28 of 292 reviews retrieved), and — this is the significant result — **proposed nothing**. `proposedForReview` is empty. Every signal beyond the raw rating was marked `insufficient_data`: no website/social data, no category/address, no review text, and explicitly that 28 of 292 reviews is too small a sample to characterize the population. Given only a partial evidence set with no web-presence signal at all, the analyst declined to recommend rather than guessing — matching rule 2 (absence of evidence is not evidence of absence) and rule 4 (propose only what the evidence supports). It could not, and did not, reproduce or contradict the operator's 73.06/100 approval; DEC-063 already established this replay cannot see the evidence that score was built from.
- Options considered: treat the Finescape mismatch as a defect and try to make the analyst's qualitative read agree with the deterministic score (rejected — the analyst is explicitly forbidden from scoring, per rule 3 and section 5; making it agree would mean either leaking scoring logic into the prompt or letting it guess, both worse than the current boundary); treat SEASONS EATS's empty `proposedForReview` as a failure to reproduce a positive result (rejected — it is evidence-honest behavior given a partial evidence set, exactly what rule 2 requires, not a defect); accept both outcomes as correct behavior of a bounded analyst operating on incomplete inputs, and record what they show about the human-then-deterministic-code authority the agent boundary is built to preserve (chosen).
- Decision: record both replay outcomes as the acceptance evidence for AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md step 4. No code change follows from this decision — nothing failed. The one actionable implication: `reputation-scoring-v1` remaining unimplemented (DEC-050) is not a cosmetic gap. This replay shows concretely that an agent's plausible-sounding qualitative read of partial evidence can diverge from what the real deterministic model would have said, in either direction — over-optimistic for Finescape, silent (correctly) for SEASONS EATS. Any future work that lets an agent's output influence a real prospect must keep `run_deterministic_scoring` as a HORUS-owned, unimplemented-by-the-agent computation, never an agent estimate presented as equivalent.
- Consequences: this closes AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md step 4 for both retained cases, one fully and one partially per DEC-063's documented gap. Steps 5 onward (comparing shadow-mode output against operator judgment in a structured, repeatable way; deciding whether to route any real future task through this runtime) remain open and unscheduled. The four unimplemented `ANALYST_TOOLS` (`inspect_public_website_readonly`, `run_deterministic_scoring`, `save_agent_draft`, `request_operator_review`) and IPC/UI wiring remain exactly as open as before this decision — this replay used only `read_evidence_snapshot`, the one tool that has ever been implemented.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-065 — The analyst boundary is reachable from the app, not only from a terminal script

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — verified live inside the packaged app (`npm run dev`, real Electron window, real IPC round trip). Lint 0/0, 74/74 tests passing (including the 4 new `analyst-ipc.test.ts` cases and 2 new `persistence.test.ts` cases), clean `tsc -b`/`vite build`/`tsc -p tsconfig.electron.json`. The panel opened, correctly reported "No retained evidence in the local store yet" — this app's own store has never had a real search run against it — and the rest of the existing representative workflow (visible in the same screenshot: outreach review, approvals, audit strip) rendered unaffected.
- Context: through DEC-064, the analyst runtime had only ever been exercised from standalone scripts (`run-analyst-live-check.ts`, `run-shadow-replay.ts`) or a hand-typed `claude -p` command. AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md's own premise is that this becomes something the operator can use during a real run, which means it has to be reachable from the Electron app itself.
- Options considered: leave the boundary terminal-only until a real prospect run needs it (defers the question of what the IPC/UI contract should look like indefinitely, and means the first time it's wired up would be under the pressure of a live prospect); wire it in fully, including letting the analyst's `proposedForReview` output write directly into a workflow state (rejected outright — DEC-045 assigns state transitions and approvals to HORUS code, never to agent output, and doing this now would be inventing a save/approval path with no operator review step behind it); wire in a read-only surface — list retained evidence, run the analyst, display its output — that performs no write of any kind (chosen).
- Decision: `electron/persistence.ts` gains `listRawSnapshots(limit?)`, a read-only, payload-free listing (id, source, retrievedAt only — never the payload, which stays behind `read_evidence_snapshot`'s own separate read-only guarantee). `electron/agent/analyst-ipc.ts` adds `runOpportunityAnalyst`, a small, dependency-injected wiring function (same pattern as `SpawnImpl`/`PrepareIsolatedWorkingDirectory`) that builds the task, guards it with `assertTaskIsBounded` before contacting any runtime, runs it, and pipes the result through `parseAnalystOutput` before ever returning it — so a malformed or evidence-fabricating output cannot reach the renderer even if a caller forgets to validate. `main.ts` wires three IPC channels (`agent:analyst:list-evidence`, `agent:analyst:availability`, `agent:analyst:run`) using the app's own evidence store — whose write path has always produced absolute `storage_path` values, so no `evidenceBasePath` workaround is needed the way DEC-062/063 required for the legacy Phase 5 database. `preload.cts` exposes these as `window.horus.agent`. `App.tsx` adds a collapsed-by-default "Opportunity analyst" panel: pick evidence by checkbox, run, see observations/proposed-for-review/missing-information, or see a failure reason. It performs no other action — no save button, no "accept" — because there is nothing yet for it to write to.
- Consequences: this is a read surface, not a decision surface. It does not implement `save_agent_draft` or `request_operator_review` (still 2 of the 4 unimplemented `ANALYST_TOOLS`) — those would require designing an actual persisted-draft/review-request record and a corresponding approval gate, which is deliberately out of scope for this decision. `run_deterministic_scoring` remains blocked on `reputation-scoring-v1` not existing (DEC-050). `inspect_public_website_readonly` also remains unimplemented; adding it would extend `evidence-mcp-server.ts` with a second real tool and is a reasonable next increment but is not part of this decision. This has not yet been run live inside the packaged Electron app — only unit-tested (`persistence.test.ts`'s new cases, `analyst-ipc.test.ts`) against fakes. A live in-app run, with real retained evidence, real Claude Code, and a screenshot or description of the panel's actual behavior, is the verification this decision needs before it can move to accepted.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-066 — `inspect_public_website_readonly` implemented, with an explicitly unresolved SSRF gap

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — unit-verified with a real local HTTP server (80/80 tests, lint clean); live verification of an actual Claude Code run invoking this tool through `--mcp-config` remains open, tracked as a known gap rather than blocking acceptance of the implementation itself.
- Context: of the 4 `ANALYST_TOOLS` named in DEC-049 with no real implementation, `inspect_public_website_readonly` is the only one that does not require a new state-owning subsystem first (`run_deterministic_scoring` needs `reputation-scoring-v1`, DEC-050; `save_agent_draft`/`request_operator_review` need a designed persisted-draft/approval record). It is also the only one of the four that reaches the live network on the analyst's behalf, which is a different risk shape than reading bytes HORUS already retrieved.
- Options considered: skip full SSRF hardening entirely and just fetch whatever URL is given (rejected — trivially lets an evidence-embedded or analyst-chosen URL reach `http://169.254.169.254/` or similar); build full SSRF hardening (resolve DNS first, check the resulting IP against private ranges, re-check on every redirect hop) before shipping anything (deferred — real engineering effort disproportionate to a single-operator internal tool whose URLs come from evidence HORUS itself retrieved, not from an untrusted multi-tenant caller); ship a bounded but not fully SSRF-hardened version with the gap explicitly documented, https-only, GET-only, hostname-pattern-blocked for the obvious local/internal cases, timeout-capped, size-capped (chosen).
- Decision: new `electron/agent/website-inspector.ts` exports `inspectPublicWebsiteReadOnly(url, options)`. It rejects non-`https:` schemes and a denylist of literal hostname patterns (`localhost`, loopback, the three private IPv4 ranges, link-local, IPv6 loopback/unique-local) before making any request; enforces a 15s default timeout via `AbortController` and a 500KB default body cap; returns status code, content-type, and text (always inert — never parsed, evaluated, or rendered). `evidence-mcp-server.ts` registers it as a second tool alongside `read_evidence_snapshot`. `evidence-tool-wiring.ts`'s `toolNameMap` gets a second entry, so a task naming `inspect_public_website_readonly` in `allowedTools` now resolves to a real capability instead of `--permission-mode dontAsk` silently denying it (DEC-058).
- **Stated limitation, per this project's own convention of not hiding uncertainty:** the hostname check is a literal-string denylist, not DNS-aware. A hostname that resolves to a private or internal address only at connection time — DNS rebinding, a misconfigured internal DNS record, a redirect to such a hostname — would pass this check and only fail if the underlying connection itself is refused or times out. This is not solved here. It is proportionate to what HORUS is today (one operator, fetching URLs drawn from evidence HORUS itself already retrieved), not to a general-purpose or multi-tenant use of this tool.
- Consequences: verified with `website-inspector.test.ts` against a real local HTTP server (not a mocked `fetch`) — confirms GET-only, https/hostname rejection before any request is made, real truncation at a byte boundary, and a real timeout actually firing rather than hanging. Wiring this in surfaced a genuine regression in an existing DEC-059 test: `agent-boundary.test.ts`'s "allow-lists exactly the mapped evidence tool" assertion had hardcoded a single expected tool name, so `--allowedTools` now correctly listing two tools (both `read_evidence_snapshot` and `inspect_public_website_readonly`, since `task()`'s default evidence defaults to `ANALYST_TOOLS`'s full list) failed a stale expectation, not a real defect. Fixed by asserting the sorted set of both mapped tool names, with a comment explaining why the other three `ANALYST_TOOLS` still resolve to nothing per DEC-058. 80/80 tests pass, lint clean. Not yet verified live: no run has confirmed Claude Code can actually select and invoke this tool through the `--mcp-config`/`--allowedTools` mechanism the way DEC-059's live check confirmed for `read_evidence_snapshot`. That would require a task whose evidence or instruction prompts the analyst to use it, which the current `buildKickoffPrompt` and `ANALYST_INSTRUCTION` do not specifically do. `run_deterministic_scoring`, `save_agent_draft`, and `request_operator_review` remain unimplemented.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-067 — `save_agent_draft` persists validated output only; `request_operator_review` needs no separate tool

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: accepted by Javier Napoles, 2026-08-07 — unit-verified (83/83 tests, lint clean, clean build) and confirmed live in the packaged app: the panel showed "Saved drafts (0)" under the evidence picker, with the rest of the representative workflow (stage 10, 14 durable events, both approvals) unaffected. A save with real evidence has not yet been observed live, since this app's own store still has no retained evidence to run the analyst against — recorded as an open follow-up, not a blocker to acceptance.
- Context: operator's direction (asked directly rather than assumed, since this creates a new persisted record): save a validated analyst draft into the local store, new table, visible in the existing panel. The remaining design question was where in the pipeline the save happens.
- Options considered, `save_agent_draft`: give the live Claude Code subprocess a real, write-capable MCP tool it can call mid-run (rejected — `evidence-mcp-server.ts` and every tool on it today are read-only by construction; a write tool available to the subprocess would let output reach storage before `parseAnalystOutput` ever inspects it, undermining the one gate this whole architecture is built around — that nothing from a run is trusted until it has been schema-checked and evidence-checked); have the renderer trigger a separate explicit "save" action after reviewing the result (adds a step without an obvious reason to withhold saving something that already passed validation, and DEC-045 already permits an agent to "prepare structured drafts" without requiring a further gate before that preparation is recorded); persist automatically, inside `runOpportunityAnalyst`, immediately after `parseAnalystOutput` accepts the result, via an injected `saveDraft` callback — the same dependency-injection shape as every other side effect in this codebase (chosen).
- Options considered, `request_operator_review`: build a second write path — a distinct "review requested" record or flag the analyst sets (rejected — `AnalystOutput.proposedForReview` already is that signal: it's a required, schema-validated field, and every entry in it must cite real evidence, per `parseAnalystOutput`'s existing checks; a separate tool would just be a second way to say the same thing, with none of the existing validation guarding it); treat `proposedForReview` as satisfying what this tool would have done, and leave the tool name itself permanently unmapped in `evidence-tool-wiring.ts` (chosen).
- Decision: `persistence.ts` gains an `agent_drafts` table and `saveAgentDraft`/`listAgentDrafts`. `analyst-ipc.ts`'s `runOpportunityAnalyst` takes an optional `saveDraft` callback, called only after `parseAnalystOutput` succeeds, never before, never with the raw unvalidated run output, and returns the resulting `draftId` (or `null` if no callback was supplied — scripts like `run-shadow-replay.ts` still don't persist anything, which is correct: those run against evidence and cases outside this app's own store). `main.ts` wires `store.saveAgentDraft` into the existing `agent:analyst:run` handler and adds `agent:analyst:list-drafts`. `App.tsx`'s analyst panel shows a running list of saved drafts and confirms a save with the draft id, explicitly labelled "a record, not an approval or a state change." `request_operator_review` gets no new code: it remains permanently unmapped in `evidence-tool-wiring.ts`'s `toolNameMap`, so naming it in a task's `allowedTools` continues to resolve to no access under DEC-058 — and that's fine, because a proposal for review has always had to travel through `proposedForReview`, which is validated regardless.
- Consequences: `save_agent_draft` and `request_operator_review` are no longer open gaps in the sense DEC-049 first recorded them, but neither exists as a literal MCP tool the subprocess can call — both are satisfied by the existing validate-then-persist pipeline instead. That leaves exactly one of the original 4 unimplemented `ANALYST_TOOLS`: `run_deterministic_scoring`, still correctly blocked on `reputation-scoring-v1` not existing (DEC-050). Verified: `persistence.test.ts` (new draft save/list case), `analyst-ipc.test.ts` (draft saved only on success, never on failure or invalid output, exact payload asserted). Not yet verified live in the running app — that requires the operator to run a real analyst task and confirm a draft appears in the saved-drafts list.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-068 — `reputation-scoring-v1` is now real code, not just a charter table

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat**, after review of the implementation, the interpretive choices below, and the 99/99 sandboxed test result. Confirming the full suite on the operator's own macOS machine remains outstanding and is not a condition of this acceptance, per the decision's own stated limitation.
- Context: DEC-050 recorded that charter section 9's reputation model existed only on paper — the only reputation figure in the codebase was the literal `score: 82` in the representative fixture, and the 30-business calibration and SEASONS EATS's 73.06 lower bound were computed outside the repository and could not be regenerated from it. That gap blocks one of `AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md`'s own acceptance criteria ("deterministic scores match recomputation from the stored inputs") and is the reason `run_deterministic_scoring` is the one `ANALYST_TOOLS` entry still unimplemented after DEC-067. `web-opportunity-v2` was confirmed still fully implemented in `src/domain/web-opportunity-audit.ts` before starting, so this decision covers reputation only.
- Options considered: implement reputation scoring as part of a broader Phase 6-adjacent batch alongside other gaps; implement it as its own bounded, reviewable change mirroring `web-opportunity-audit.ts`'s existing conventions (`Measurement<T>`, per-factor evidence arrays, a model-version constant, one pure function); leave the gap recorded and wait for a later phase.
- Decision: `src/domain/reputation-scoring.ts` implements charter section 9 in full — six gates (G1–G6), the five scoring factors with their exact formulas, the 9.6 missing-data principle, and the 9.4/9.5 split between objective auto-rejects and judgment-dependent operator flags. Reused `web-opportunity-audit.ts`'s `Measurement<T>` type rather than redefining it. Specific interpretive choices, made explicit because the charter does not fully resolve them:
  - **G4 (complaint pattern) never produces `autoReject`.** Charter 9.1 lists it as a gate that "must pass," but 9.5 lists "recurring serious complaints" as a judgment-dependent operator flag, and DEC-008 is explicit that judgment signals never auto-reject. Resolved by having a failed G4 fail the gate (so `qualified` becomes `false`, matching 9.1) while keeping `autoReject` strictly reserved for the objective 9.4 list — closed/permanently-closed (G5), confirmed mismatch or duplicate (G6, duplicate id), out-of-market, and G1–G3 failing on complete data. A failed G4 is surfaced through `flags` with its evidence, same as every other judgment signal; nothing here treats it as final, since this module only computes a score — it does not gate publication, outreach, or any other consequential action (rule 9, `AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md`).
  - **G4–G6 are caller-supplied assessments, not computed.** Detecting a "serious recurring complaint pattern," confirming operational status, or confirming listing identity all require reading review text or comparing addresses — judgment or retrieval work this module does not do. The function takes each as a `{ status; evidence }` input requiring non-empty evidence, and computes only what charter 9.2's formulas are actually formulas for (F1–F5) plus the objective parts of G1–G3.
  - **Franchise ownership, review-manipulation, and similar 9.5 signals are not detected.** CURRENT_STATE.md already records these as undefined capabilities. Rather than fabricate a detection rule, `additionalOperatorFlags` passes caller-supplied flags through verbatim with their evidence.
  - **Only final displayed points are rounded (9.2)** — factor scores are cleared of binary floating-point noise (e.g., `4.8 − 4.5` is not exactly `0.3` in IEEE 754, which without correction silently turned a charter-table 29.8 into 29.7) to 6 decimal places internally, not rounded to 1 decimal, so nothing downstream loses precision the charter says to keep.
  - A recent-consistency input with fewer than 5 trailing-year reviews throws rather than silently computing a Factor 4 score, since charter 9.2 requires that minimum and a caller should mark it `unmeasured` instead.
- Consequences: verified against every one of charter 9.3's six reference profiles (all match to the table's own precision — two exposed the floating-point rounding issue above, fixed at the source rather than in the test) and against three real numbers already published in `CURRENT_STATE.md`'s 2026-08-05 review-volume saturation check (Tuff Lawn 314 reviews → 22.8 pts, CINCO DE MAYO 392 → 24.8, Teed and Brown 435 → 25.0 — all reproduce exactly). Also independently confirmed CINCO DE MAYO's real cached SerpApi listing (`cache/raw/serpapi/google_maps/2026-08-05T23-55-57Z_restaurants-stamford-ct-page-2.json`) reports rating 4.8 and 392 reviews, consistent with the calibration record. `tests/reputation-scoring.test.ts` adds 16 tests covering the reference profiles, the calibration regression, the 9.6 missing-data principle for G3 (proven both directions — a partial sample that already proves enough passes, one that does not stays `insufficient_data` rather than failing), the objective/judgment auto-reject split, the insufficient-data short-circuit, and the below-5-reviews guard.

  **What was verified live, in this environment:** the mounted repository's `node_modules` were built on the operator's macOS machine; this session runs in a sandboxed Linux ARM64 container, and reinstalling in place would have overwritten the operator's real `node_modules` with binaries that do not run on macOS. To avoid that, verification ran in an isolated `/tmp` copy of the repository, never touching the mounted repository's actual `node_modules`. A first `npm install` there failed rebuilding `better-sqlite3`'s native binding (no network access to `nodejs.org` for Node headers); `npm install --ignore-scripts` was tried next expecting it to skip that native dependency entirely, but the resulting install could still load `better-sqlite3` successfully — evidently a prebuilt binding was already resolvable without a local compile — so the full suite could in fact run. Confirmed: `oxlint` clean (0 errors across `src electron tests`), `tsc -b` clean, `tsc -p tsconfig.electron.json` clean, `vite build` clean, and **99 of 99 tests passing across all 14 test files** — the 83 tests present as of DEC-067 (including `persistence.test.ts`, `agent-boundary.test.ts`, `evidence-store.test.ts`, `node-spawn.test.ts`, `analyst-ipc.test.ts`, `workflow-state.test.ts`, `agent-working-directory.test.ts`, `website-inspector.test.ts`) plus this decision's 16 new `reputation-scoring.test.ts` cases, none touched by this change and all still passing. This was run against an isolated copy in a different OS/architecture than the operator's machine, not the mounted repository itself — a first run of `npm run lint && npm run test && npm run build` on the real macOS machine remains the one verification step this decision could not itself perform.
- Consequences (continued): this resolves DEC-050's finding for reputation specifically — a deterministic reputation score can now be recomputed from stored inputs, and the historical CURRENT_STATE.md and checkpoint figures (Finescape 48.1, SEASONS EATS 73.06, the 30-business calibration set) remain recorded history but are **not** reproduced by this decision, since the original per-business inputs (exact `r365`, `daysSinceLatestReview`, `recentConsistency`, `historySpanYears` per business) were not re-extracted from the raw calibration cache here — only the review-volume figures were spot-checked, because that was the only input the charter's own calibration notes published with enough precision to check against. Reproducing the full 30-business set from `cache/raw/serpapi/` against this implementation, and updating `run_deterministic_scoring` in the agent boundary to call it, are the natural next steps and are explicitly not done by this decision. Nothing here changes any approval gate, publishes anything, or contacts anyone.
- Supersedes: not applicable — narrows DEC-050's finding to "resolved for reputation-scoring-v1, still open for wiring it into `run_deterministic_scoring` and for reproducing the historical calibration set."

### DEC-069 — Real SerpApi discovery is wired to the interface, as its own screen

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** ("continua que tu eres el que sabe" — proceed, delegating judgment on subsequent steps). No real SerpApi credit was spent under this decision; that remains the operator's own choice, unchanged by acceptance.
- Context: `electron/integrations/serpapi.ts` has worked, and been tested, since Phase 3 — but `App.tsx`'s `SearchStage` never called it. Pressing "Run representative search" only advanced `VerticalWorkflowState` to the next fixture stage with a hardcoded message. That stage sits inside the Phase 4 representative workflow, whose own on-screen notice says plainly: "This flow uses a fictional, source-free representative case. Search, public deployment, Gmail opening, and sending are not executed here." Also found while starting this work: no code anywhere reads `config/local.json` — the file DEC-035 defines to hold the real `serpapi_key` — so even a wired call would have had no credential to use.
- Options considered, put to the operator directly because both carry real consequences (spending a scarce, capped SerpApi credit; DEC-019/DEC-032): make `SearchStage` itself call the real module, updating its notice since the representative flow would no longer be search-safe; or add a new, separate "Real discovery search" surface outside the representative workflow, leaving the fixture flow and its notice exactly as true as they are today. **The operator chose the second.** A related second question — whether to spend a real credit in this session to verify the path live — was left to judgment; given DEC-019's scarcity (159 of 250 free searches were remaining as of the last reconciliation) and this project's consistent pattern of treating a real external retrieval as its own deliberate, later decision (DEC-018's discovery risk-closing was verified against documentation, not a live spend; Phase 5's real search was its own separately-authorized step), this was implemented and tested against a fake `fetch` only. No real SerpApi credit was spent by this decision.
- Decision:
  - `electron/config.ts` (new) reads and validates `config/local.json` on demand, converting its snake_case fields to typed camelCase and throwing named errors (`OperatorConfigMissing`, `OperatorConfigInvalid`) rather than silently defaulting. `requireSerpApiKey` is a separate, later check — a config file can exist and be well-formed while still shipping `config/local.json.example`'s empty credential strings, and the error for that specific case should name exactly what's missing.
  - `electron/discovery-ipc.ts` (new) wires `executeSerpApiDiscovery` to a single, dependency-injected function (`appendRawSnapshot`, `fetchImpl`, `now` all passed in, same pattern as `analyst-ipc.ts`). It performs exactly one discovery request, stores its raw response as evidence before returning anything (DEC-020), and returns a sanitized candidate list — `title`/`rating`/`reviews`/`type`/`data_id`/`address`/`website` read defensively, a missing field left `null` rather than guessed at. The credential never leaves `config.ts` and `serpapi.ts`; what reaches storage and the renderer is the DEC-046 credential-redacted `requestUrl`.
  - `main.ts` adds one IPC channel, `discovery:run`, resolving `config/local.json` from the compiled `build/electron/` output path back to the repository root (`../../../..`, matching the layout DEC-051 fixed) and loading it fresh on every call rather than caching it at startup — a single-operator local tool with no reason to require a restart after editing its own config file.
  - `App.tsx` adds `RealDiscoverySearch`, a new top-level, collapsed-by-default section rendered alongside (not inside) the representative `stages` workflow. It states its own scope plainly — one request, no pagination to `MAX_EXAMINED`, no gates, no scoring — and requires an explicit "I understand this spends a real SerpApi credit" checkbox before its run button enables, distinct from (and not a substitute for) the two DEC-004 approval gates, which remain publication and outreach only.
- Consequences: closes the gap as scoped — a real search button now reaches the real integration and real evidence storage — but does not implement the full charter-12 search loop (pagination to `TARGET_QUALIFIED`/`MAX_EXAMINED`), does not apply gates G1/G2 before showing candidates, and does not run either scoring model against the results. Those remain explicitly open, named in the new panel's own on-screen text so the gap is visible to the operator, not just to this document. `tests/config.test.ts` (7 tests) and `tests/discovery-ipc.test.ts` (5 tests) cover config parsing/validation and the discovery wiring (credential never reaching storage or the returned result, the 20-result page cap, a failed request returning a typed failure rather than throwing or storing anything, an empty `local_results` array producing zero candidates rather than an error). Verified in the same isolated `/tmp` copy DEC-068 used, for the same reason (the mounted repository's `node_modules` is built for macOS): `oxlint` clean, `tsc -b` clean, `tsc -p tsconfig.electron.json` clean, `vite build` clean and confirmed `config.js`/`discovery-ipc.js` compiled next to `main.js` at the expected path, and **111 of 111 tests passing** (99 from DEC-068 plus these 12 new ones). No real SerpApi request was made — the network path is verified only against a fake `fetch`, and the config path is verified only against synthetic temporary files, never the operator's real `config/local.json`. The first real run, spending the first real credit through this new code path, is the operator's decision and has not happened yet.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-070 — Discovery candidates get a G1/G2 quick screen, not a fabricated score

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** (delegated: "continua que tu eres el que sabe"), following the same isolated-sandbox verification as DEC-068/DEC-069.
- Context: after DEC-069 wired real discovery, its candidate list showed raw listing fields (rating, review count) with no connection at all to `reputation-scoring-v1` (DEC-068) — the two pieces of work sat side by side, unconnected, in the same session. Calling `buildReputationScore` directly against a bare discovery listing would not have helped: with no review-history retrieval performed, every input past rating/review count is `unmeasured`, and the function correctly, immediately returns `insufficient_data` for the whole result (charter 9.4's explicit rule) — technically honest, but it would hide the two gates (G1, G2) that a bare listing's own fields already answer.
- Options considered: leave discovery and scoring unconnected until a future review-history retrieval feature exists; call `buildReputationScore` anyway and accept that it always reports `insufficient_data` for every fresh discovery candidate; add a narrower function that answers only the two gates a bare listing can actually answer, and say plainly that it is not qualification.
- Decision: `screenListingGates` (new, exported from `src/domain/reputation-scoring.ts`) takes `{ rating, reviewCount }` and returns G1 and G2 only, reusing the exact same internal gate logic `buildReputationScore` uses (`evaluateRatingGate`, `evaluateVolumeGate`) rather than duplicating the thresholds. It never returns a `qualified` field, a score, or anything resembling one. The `RealDiscoverySearch` panel (DEC-069) now shows each candidate's G1/G2 status inline, with an explicit on-screen statement that G3–G6 and all five scoring factors require review-history retrieval this screen does not perform.
- Consequences: this is the first point in the codebase where a real discovery result and `reputation-scoring-v1` touch at all — a small connection, deliberately smaller than "wire scoring into discovery," because the data a full score needs does not exist yet at this stage of the pipeline. The next real increment is a review-history retrieval step (a further SerpApi cost per candidate, per DEC-018/DEC-019) that would let `buildReputationScore` run for real rather than always reporting `insufficient_data`; that is not implemented here. 1 new test (`screenListingGates` — pass/fail/insufficient_data) brings the suite to 112, verified the same way as DEC-068/DEC-069 (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vite build`, tests all clean in the isolated sandbox; not yet re-confirmed on the operator's machine).
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-071 — Review-history retrieval closes the loop: a real end-to-end reputation score

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** (delegated: "sigue" / "continua que tu eres el que sabe"), verified the same isolated-sandbox way as DEC-068–070.
- Context: DEC-070 connected discovery to `reputation-scoring-v1` only at the two gates a bare listing can answer (G1/G2). Everything past that — G3's recency, Factor 3's recency, Factor 4's consistency — needs actual review timestamps, which the discovery request never retrieves (it returns listing aggregates, not individual reviews). Closing that gap needs a second, separate SerpApi surface: `google_maps_reviews`, keyed by the listing's `data_id`, paginated newest-first (confirmed against real cached Phase 1 evidence, `cache/raw/serpapi/google_maps/*reviews*.json` — the exact request shape DEC-018 described from documentation, now also confirmed from a real captured response: `engine=google_maps_reviews`, `sort_by=newestFirst`, `serpapi_pagination.next_page_token` for continuation, each review's `iso_date`/`rating` fields as needed).
- Options considered: leave discovery and full scoring disconnected until a dedicated future phase; retrieve full review history unconditionally per candidate (expensive, and Factor 5 explicitly does not need it per DEC-018's own cost reasoning); retrieve up to a small page cap per candidate, on demand, per candidate, marking anything not reached as `partial_data`/`insufficient_data` rather than guessed.
- Decision:
  - `electron/integrations/serpapi.ts` gains `buildSerpApiReviewsPlan`/`executeSerpApiReviews`, mirroring the discovery functions' credential-redaction pattern (DEC-046) exactly.
  - `electron/review-retrieval-ipc.ts` (new) paginates up to a default 3 pages (matching the page count Phase 1 calibration actually used before treating further pagination as its own decision), storing every page as its own immutable raw snapshot before returning anything, and reports `paginationExhausted` — true only if a page truly had no further token, never merely because the cap was hit. A page failure keeps whatever was already stored; it does not roll evidence back.
  - `src/domain/review-history.ts` (new) is a pure function, `summarizeReviewHistory`, turning raw `{isoDate, rating}` pairs plus `retrievedAt` and `paginationExhausted` into exactly the shape `reputation-scoring-v1`'s `recentActivity`/`recentConsistency` inputs need — computed against the stored retrieval timestamp per charter 9.7, never the current clock. It marks the 90-day and 365-day sample-completeness windows independently, since a review already older than 90 days proves that window complete on its own even mid-page-cap (charter 9.6).
  - The renderer, not the main process, calls `buildReputationScore` — `CandidateScoreAction` in `App.tsx` fetches review history via the new IPC channel, summarizes it, and builds a real `ReputationScore` per candidate. This follows the same layering DEC-070 already established (`screenListingGates` runs in the renderer too) rather than importing `src/domain` into `electron/`, which `tsconfig.electron.json`'s `rootDir` does not permit.
  - Longevity (Factor 5) is deliberately left `unmeasured` with a reason citing DEC-018's stated reasoning: a 5-point factor is not worth paying for full-history pagination.
  - G4 (complaint pattern), G5 (operational status), and G6 (listing identity) are deliberately left `insufficient_data` rather than defaulted to passing — this component has no way to read reviews for a complaint pattern, confirm the listing is still operating, or confirm its identity, and per DEC-008 those require operator judgment, not an invented default. The practical effect: `qualified` can come back `false` even when every retrievable factor looks strong, because the operator is the missing gate, exactly as charter 9.1 and DEC-008 intend.
- Consequences: for the first time, a real discovered candidate can receive a real, evidence-backed `reputation-scoring-v1` result computed from data this session actually retrieved — not a fixture, not a charter-table exercise. It is still bounded: 3 pages per candidate is a real, further SerpApi cost (on top of the one discovery credit), and nothing here paginates further automatically or retries past the cap without the operator asking again. 18 new tests (`review-history.test.ts` — 7, `review-retrieval-ipc.test.ts` — 4, 2 new cases in `integration-contracts.test.ts`) bring the suite to 125, all passing in the same isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vite build` all clean; not yet re-confirmed on the operator's own machine). No real SerpApi credit was spent — every test uses a fake `fetch`. The first real end-to-end run, from a real search through a real score, is the operator's own next step.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-072 — Web-opportunity gets two real, mechanical factors; the other three stay honestly unmeasured

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** (selected directly: "Web-opportunity real (recomendado)" from a menu of next-step options), verified the same isolated-sandbox way as DEC-068–071.
- Context: `web-opportunity-v2` (`src/domain/web-opportunity-audit.ts`) has been fully implemented since before this session (DEC-050 confirmed it), but nothing in the app ever called it with real measurements — same shape of gap DEC-068/069/071 already closed for reputation. Of its five factors, only `load_performance` has an existing, documented real measurement source: PageSpeed Insights (DEC-012), already planned but never executed (`buildPageSpeedMobilePlan` had no matching `execute*` function, unlike `serpapi.ts`). The other four factors — mobile responsiveness, obsolete appearance (7 indicators), broken elements (a link crawl plus contact-path execution), commercial ineffectiveness (6 content-search indicators) — all require either rendering a page or judging its content, neither of which a raw HTTP fetch can honestly claim to do.
- Options considered: leave web-opportunity entirely disconnected from real candidates until a future phase; approximate the other four factors with text-based heuristics on fetched HTML (regex for a viewport tag, keyword search for services, etc.); wire only what is genuinely mechanical — performance via real PageSpeed, plus the one obsolete-appearance indicator (`no-https`) knowable from the URL's own scheme with no judgment involved — and leave the rest explicitly `unmeasured`.
- Decision: the third option. Specifically:
  - `electron/integrations/pagespeed.ts` gains `executePageSpeedMobile`, mirroring `executeSerpApiDiscovery`'s credential-redaction pattern (DEC-046) — the PageSpeed `key` parameter is redacted the same way SerpApi's `api_key` is.
  - `electron/web-opportunity-ipc.ts` (new) runs two independent checks per candidate website and never lets one's failure block the other: (1) real PageSpeed, extracting `lighthouseResult.audits.interactive.numericValue` (confirmed against real cached Phase 1 PageSpeed evidence, `cache/raw/pagespeed/*.json`) as the Time to Interactive `load_performance` needs; (2) `servesHttps`, computed from the URL's own protocol with **no network call at all** — fully mechanical, zero ambiguity; and, only when `servesHttps` is true (the read-only inspector this reuses requires https), a `tel:`-link check via `inspect_public_website_readonly` (DEC-066's existing, already-tested tool, reused rather than reimplemented).
  - The `tel:`-link finding is surfaced to the operator as supplementary evidence, explicitly **not** folded into the `brokenElements` factor — one regex match on one fetched page is not the checked-links crawl that factor's `checkedLinks`/`brokenLinks` fields are meant to represent, and scoring it as if it were would overstate confidence (the same reasoning DEC-070 applied to reputation's G4–G6).
  - `App.tsx`'s `CandidateWebOpportunityAction` calls `buildWebOpportunityAudit` with `performance` and `obsoleteAppearance` (only the `no-https` indicator, when applicable) as `measured`, and `mobile`/`brokenElements`/`commercialIneffectiveness` as `unmeasured` with reasons naming exactly what would need to be built to measure them for real.
- Consequences: `web-opportunity-v2` now produces a real, evidence-backed `scoreLowerBound` from actual retrieved data for the first time — genuinely a lower bound, not an approximation dressed as a full audit, since 3 of 5 factors are honestly absent rather than guessed. `runWebOpportunityMeasurement` stores every real retrieval (PageSpeed response, fetched page excerpt) as immutable evidence via `appendRawSnapshot`, same as every other real retrieval path in this codebase. 8 new tests (`web-opportunity-ipc.test.ts` — 5, `PAGESPEED_CREDENTIAL_PLACEHOLDER`/`executePageSpeedMobile` cases in `integration-contracts.test.ts` — 1) bring the suite to 131, all passing in the same isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vite build` clean; not yet re-confirmed on the operator's own machine). No real PageSpeed request was made — every test uses a fake `fetch`. Mobile responsiveness, the remaining six obsolete-appearance indicators, a real broken-link crawl, and commercial ineffectiveness remain open, and would each need either a rendering capability this app does not have or a judgment call this module deliberately does not fabricate.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-073 — DEC-069 through DEC-072 confirmed live, in the packaged app, spending real credits

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** — this decision is itself the record of that live verification, not a proposal awaiting it.
- Context: DEC-069 through DEC-072 were each verified only in an isolated `/tmp` sandbox copy against a fake `fetch`, never against the real packaged app, the operator's real `config/local.json`, or a real network call — stated as an open gap in each of those decisions. Consistent with this project's own repeated finding (DEC-058, DEC-060, DEC-061: reading and unit-testing find shapes, running finds what the shapes don't cover), that gap was closed by actually running it.
- What happened: the operator ran `npm install && npm run dev` in `apps/operator` on their own machine, opened the packaged Electron app, and worked the new panels directly.
  1. **Real discovery** (DEC-069): a real landscaping/Norwalk search returned 20 real candidates, stored as evidence snapshot `raw_178dc9bb-62fe-4bda-9999-8a1c582696d5` at `2026-08-07T23:33:50.752Z`. `discovery:run`'s `repoRoot` path resolution (`../../../..` from the compiled `build/electron/` output, reasoned about but never executed under DEC-069) resolved correctly against the real repository layout.
  2. **G1/G2 screen** (DEC-070): displayed correctly per real candidate, including correctly suppressing the web-opportunity button and showing "No website field on this listing; nothing to measure" for LT Landscaping & Masonry, whose real listing has no website field.
  3. **Real reputation score** (DEC-071): TwoGen Landscaping (5.0 rating, 42 reviews) retrieved real review history and scored 71.1/100 — `average_rating` 35.0/35, `review_volume` 4.7/25, `review_recency` 16.4/20, `recent_consistency` 15.0/15, `longevity_evidence` 0.0/5 (flagged `longevity_unknown`, exactly as designed since full-history retrieval is deliberately not performed). G1–G3 passed on real data; G4–G6 correctly `insufficient_data`; **`qualified: false` despite a score above the 70 threshold** — the first live confirmation that the operator-judgment gates actually withhold qualification rather than being silently satisfied by a good score, which was the entire point of DEC-071's design.
  4. **Real web-opportunity measurement** (DEC-072): the same candidate's site measured `load_performance` 8.2/12 from a real PageSpeed request, `obsolete_appearance` 0.0/20 measured (confirmed https, no indicator raised), `mobile_responsiveness`/`broken_elements`/`commercial_ineffectiveness` correctly `unmeasured`, `scoreLowerBound` 8.2/100 correctly marked `partial_data`, and a real `tel:` link found and shown as an unscored supplementary finding, not folded into `broken_elements` — exactly as DEC-072 specified.
- Consequences: nothing found here contradicted any of DEC-069–072's design. No code change follows from this decision — it is acceptance evidence, in the same sense DEC-064 was for the shadow replay. Real SerpApi and PageSpeed credits were spent for the first time under this new code, by the operator's own action, not automatically. What remains unverified live: `runReviewHistoryRetrieval`'s multi-page pagination path (this run's history retrieval apparently completed within its evidence, `longevity_unknown` and no pagination-related flag or error appearing, but the exact page count is not visible from the screenshot alone) and a failure path (a rejected PageSpeed or SerpApi request, a missing config value) — both remain exercised only by the sandboxed fake-`fetch` tests. The four unimplemented Phase 6 charter-9/10 gaps this session opened (DEC-068 reputation, DEC-069 discovery, DEC-071 review history, DEC-072 web-opportunity) are now each confirmed at least once outside a test file.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-074 — Real proximity from data already retrieved, with home base coordinates as a one-time manual addition

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** (continuing the "sigue" delegation; MVP wiring prioritized over UI/UX for now, per explicit instruction), verified the same isolated-sandbox way as DEC-068–072, live-testing deferred at the operator's own request to not interrupt the wiring sequence.
- Context: DEC-017 requires proximity bands to rank the shortlist before web-opportunity or reputation. Nothing in the codebase computed a distance at all — no geocoding, no haversine, home base storage existed in `config.ts` (DEC-069) but nothing ever read `homeBase` after parsing it. Checking the real cached discovery evidence (`cache/raw/serpapi/google_maps/*.json`) found that SerpApi's Google Maps listings already include `gps_coordinates` (`{latitude, longitude}`) on every candidate — meaning proximity to a business needs no extra retrieval or credit at all, only a home-base coordinate to measure from.
- Options considered: add a geocoding integration (new API, new credential) to convert the configured street address to coordinates automatically; require the operator to add `latitude`/`longitude` to `config/local.json`'s `home_base` once, manually; leave proximity unimplemented until geocoding is designed properly.
- Decision: manual, one-time coordinates — the same pattern DEC-016 already established for the rest of `home_base` ("the operator configures... HORUS stores"), rather than adding a new external dependency and credential for a single lookup the operator can do once by right-clicking their own address on a map. Specifically:
  - `src/domain/proximity.ts` (new, pure): `haversineDistanceMiles`, `bandForDistanceMiles` (the charter's provisional 5/15/30-mile bands, charter §11.1), `assessProximity`, and `compareProximityBands` for ordering. Every result carries `method: 'straight_line'` — this is great-circle distance, not driving distance, exactly the gap Phase 1's checkpoint already recorded as unmeasured ("no routing capability is configured").
  - `electron/config.ts`: `home_base.latitude`/`home_base.longitude` are new, **optional** fields (`optionalNumber`), so every config file written before this decision keeps loading unchanged. `getHomeBaseCoordinates` returns `null` rather than throwing when either is unset — an unconfigured home base is a normal state, not a configuration error.
  - `electron/discovery-ipc.ts`: candidates now carry `coordinates` extracted from the listing's own `gps_coordinates`, `null` when absent — no new request, no new cost.
  - `main.ts` adds `discovery:home-base-coordinates`, returning **only the coordinate pair, never the street address, city, state, or postal code** — a deliberate minimization beyond what DEC-035 strictly requires, since the renderer has no need for the full address to compute a distance.
  - `App.tsx`'s discovery panel computes `assessProximity` per candidate in the renderer (same layering as `screenListingGates`, `buildReputationScore`, `buildWebOpportunityAudit` — domain math runs in the renderer, IO and credentials stay in the main process) and shows plain-text distance/band, explicitly deferred from any visual polish per the operator's own instruction to finish MVP wiring before UI/UX.
- Consequences: proximity is real wherever both a candidate's `gps_coordinates` and the operator's configured home-base coordinates exist; otherwise it says exactly which one is missing rather than silently omitting the column. `compareProximityBands` exists but nothing yet calls it to actually order a shortlist — DEC-017's full ranking (proximity band, then web-opportunity, then reputation as tie-break) remains unbuilt; this decision only supplies the first of its three inputs as real, working code. 20 new tests (`proximity.test.ts` — 8, `config.test.ts` +5, `discovery-ipc.test.ts` +1 assertion extension) bring the suite to 144, all passing in the isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vite build` clean). **Not yet verified live** — unlike DEC-069–072, this one was not run in the packaged app before being recorded, at the operator's own direction to keep moving through MVP pieces rather than pause for each live check; it should be exercised live before being relied on for a real ranking decision.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-075 — A real shortlist, ranked only from what has actually been measured

- Date: 2026-08-07
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-07 in chat** ("si si", continuing the MVP-first delegation), verified the same isolated-sandbox way as DEC-068–074. Not yet live-tested, same stated gap as DEC-074.
- Context: DEC-013/DEC-017 define the shortlist ordering — reputation qualifies, proximity band ranks first among qualified candidates, web-opportunity ranks within a band, reputation breaks ties. Nothing computed this. Reputation (DEC-071) and web-opportunity (DEC-072) were each scored per candidate on demand, and proximity (DEC-074) computed inline, but the three were never combined into an order, and each per-candidate score lived only in its own button's local component state, invisible to anything else on the page.
- Options considered: automatically score every discovered candidate's reputation and web-opportunity to build a complete shortlist in one step (rejected — this would spend a SerpApi review-history retrieval and a PageSpeed request per candidate, up to 20 at once, without the same per-action spend acknowledgment DEC-069/071/072 already require); build the ranking function now and only rank whatever the operator has already chosen to score by hand; defer ranking entirely until an automatic, consent-aware bulk-scoring flow is designed.
- Decision: `src/domain/shortlist.ts` (new, pure): `buildShortlist` partitions candidates into `ranked` (has reputation qualification, a proximity band, and a web-opportunity score, all three) and `excluded`, each exclusion carrying a specific reason (`not_reputation_qualified`, `no_proximity_data`, `no_web_opportunity_data`) rather than a candidate silently vanishing from the list. Ranking order: proximity band ascending, then web-opportunity score descending within a band (DEC-013: weaker web presence — higher opportunity score — ranks higher), then reputation score descending as the final tie-break. `App.tsx` lifts the per-candidate `ReputationScore`/`WebOpportunityAudit` state that DEC-071/072's action components previously kept locally up into `RealDiscoverySearch`, via `onScored`/`onMeasured` callbacks, and a new `ShortlistView` renders `buildShortlist`'s result live as the operator scores candidates by hand — no new retrieval, no new cost, purely a read of what already exists in memory.
- Consequences: for the first time, candidates the operator has scored are actually ordered per the charter's own rule, not just individually displayed. This does not implement automatic bulk scoring — the shortlist only grows as the operator clicks through DEC-071/072's per-candidate, credit-spending actions, which remains a deliberate choice given the credit-cost concerns DEC-019/032 already established. 8 new tests (`shortlist.test.ts`) bring the suite to 152, all passing in the isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vite build` clean). Not yet exercised live in the packaged app — deferred at the operator's own instruction to keep moving through MVP pieces before returning to verification and UI/UX.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-076 — Prospect selection consolidates evidence in memory; it persists nothing

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: **accepted, approved by the operator 2026-08-08 in chat** ("haz lo que parezca mejor", continuing the MVP-first delegation), verified the same isolated-sandbox way as DEC-068–075. Not yet live-tested, same stated gap as DEC-074/075.
- Context: DEC-075 produces a ranked, real shortlist, but the operator still had to re-read a candidate's scattered gate/factor/proximity output across separate on-page actions to weigh one entry against another. Section 4 of the charter treats "the operator picks one" as a real workflow step, not an afterthought — it deserved one consolidated, honest view of everything already measured for that candidate, without inventing a new capability.
- Options considered: add a `selectedProspect` table to SQLite now, so a pick survives a restart (rejected — a schema addition is a separate, bigger decision than a UI affordance, and nothing yet needs a pick to survive a restart, since demonstration generation and outreach don't exist yet either); render a consolidated view driven by `useState` only, discarded on app close; skip consolidation and leave the operator reading per-action output as before.
- Decision: `ProspectRecord` (new component in `App.tsx`) renders the frozen evidence already held in memory for one selected shortlist entry — raw listing fields, the full `ReputationScore` (gates, factors, flags, lower bound, qualified/not), the full `WebOpportunityAudit` (factors, flags, lower bound, status), and proximity, computed the same way `ShortlistView`/the per-candidate actions already do. `ShortlistView` gained a "Select as prospect" button per ranked entry; selecting sets `selectedProspectId` in `RealDiscoverySearch`'s state, which is passed down. Nothing is written to the SQLite evidence store or any other persistence layer — selecting a prospect is exactly as durable as the in-memory scores it summarizes, and says so on screen. The view also states explicitly, on screen, that selection is not an approval, publication, or outreach action — those remain separate, not-yet-built steps gated per DEC-004/DEC-045.
- Consequences: the operator gets one place to weigh a candidate instead of several. The cost is real: closing the app or re-running discovery loses the selection along with the rest of the in-memory scores, matching DEC-075's own limitation rather than papering over it with a persistence layer that hasn't been reasoned through. No new pure domain logic was introduced (the component only reads `ReputationScore`/`WebOpportunityAudit`/`assessProximity`, all already tested), so no new unit tests were required; the existing 152-test suite, both `tsc` passes, `oxlint`, and `vite build` all stayed clean in the isolated sandbox. Not yet exercised live in the packaged app — deferred at the operator's own instruction to keep moving through MVP pieces before returning to verification and UI/UX. A persisted "selected prospect" record, demonstration generation from this evidence, and both DEC-004 approval gates remain unbuilt and are each their own future decision.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-077 — `maxExamined` actually bounds the result; a repeat search reuses cached evidence instead of spending a credit

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: **accepted, live finding + operator-requested fix, 2026-08-08.** Verified in the isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vitest run`, `vite build` all clean). The bug half was discovered by the operator's own first live use of the field; the caching half was the operator's own explicit request in the same session ("quiero que cuando se haga una búsqueda quede guardada la data de la compañía para no desperdiciar el SerpApi credit").
- Context: two related problems surfaced in the same live session. First, the operator set "Maximum examined this request" to 5 and got 20 candidates back — SerpApi's Google Maps engine does not reliably honor the `num` query parameter for `local_results`, a limitation DEC-018 had flagged as unconfirmed pending a real run; this confirms it. Second, DEC-020 already established that every external response should be cached so a repeat retrieval never re-spends a credit by default, but discovery search never implemented that half of the rule — running the same category+city search twice always spent two credits.
- Options considered, for the cap: silently trust `num`; slice the returned list client-side to `maxExamined` after extraction. Options considered, for caching: cache with a freshness window at search time (rejected — DEC-021 already says browsing/ranking a shortlist may use cached data of any age, freshness is only enforced at the two approval gates, so a search-time expiry would be inventing a rule that doesn't exist); cache indefinitely and let the operator force a fresh search when they want current data.
- Decision: `discovery-ipc.ts`'s `extractCandidates(...).slice(0, input.maxExamined)` is applied both to a fresh SerpApi response and to a cached one, so the field means what it says regardless of source. `runRealDiscoverySearch` gained `findCachedSnapshot` (checked first, unless `forceRefresh` is set) and stores the raw evidence request as a structured `{ requestUrl, category, city }` object rather than a bare URL string, so a later search can match on category+city case-insensitively without re-parsing a redacted URL. `persistence.ts` gained `findLatestRawSnapshot({ source, matches })`, a generic predicate-based lookup over stored `request_json`, most recent first — general enough to be reused by any future cached-lookup need, not discovery-specific. `App.tsx` shows whether a result came from cache or a fresh request, and a new "force a fresh search" checkbox (unchecked by default) lets the operator explicitly spend a new credit.
- Consequences: the field now does what it says, and the first real cost saving from DEC-020's caching principle actually materializes for discovery search — re-opening a prior search, or re-running one to test a UI change, costs nothing. The cache has no expiry and no size limit; an operator wanting current listings for an already-searched category+city must check "force a fresh search" explicitly. Older snapshots stored before this decision (a bare URL string as `request`) never match the new structured predicate and are silently never served from cache — the cache starts working from this point forward, not retroactively, which is an accepted, low-cost limitation rather than a migration. 8 new/changed tests (2 in `persistence.test.ts`, 3 in `discovery-ipc.test.ts`, plus updates to the existing 2) bring the suite to 158 before DEC-078's addition.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-078 — A website screenshot, captured on request, shown only in the prospect record

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: **accepted, operator-requested, 2026-08-08**, after two clarifying questions (capture method, where shown) resolved in chat: Electron's own hidden-window capture (no new credential, no new paid service, no new dependency) and ProspectRecord only (not automatic across the whole shortlist). Verified in the isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vitest run`, `vite build` all clean). Not yet exercised live.
- Context: the operator wanted to see a candidate's website home page next to its evidence, without leaving the app or trusting a scraped text excerpt alone. Three capture approaches exist — a paid third-party screenshot API, a headless-Chromium dependency (Playwright), or Electron's own `BrowserWindow`/`capturePage`, which the app already depends on and requires nothing new.
- Options considered: automatic capture for every shortlist candidate (rejected by the operator's own answer — too many captures, too much time/cost per shortlist, for evidence the operator may never look at); a manual per-candidate button in the shortlist itself (rejected — the operator chose ProspectRecord-only, after a prospect is already selected); a manual button inside `ProspectRecord`, triggered once per selection (chosen).
- Decision: `electron/website-screenshot.ts` (new): `captureWebsiteScreenshot(url, { createWindow, timeoutMs, now })`, with `createWindow` injected so the module never imports the `electron` package and stays testable under plain Node/vitest — the same reason `node-spawn.ts` keeps its real `spawn` call out of `runtime.ts`. URL validation (https-only, local/internal hostname denylist) is pulled into a new shared `electron/agent/url-safety.ts`, identical in behavior to `website-inspector.ts`'s existing check but not refactored to share code with it, to avoid touching that file's already-verified tests for a cosmetic dedupe. `main.ts` supplies the real window factory (`show: false`, `offscreen: true` — nothing appears on the operator's screen) and a new `discovery:capture-screenshot` IPC channel; `preload.cts`/`global.d.ts` expose `window.horus.discovery.captureScreenshot`. `ProspectRecord` gained a "Capture website screenshot" button, shown only when the candidate has a website field, rendering the returned data URL directly.
- Consequences: capturing costs no credit and needs no new credential, at the cost of being less robust than a dedicated screenshot service against sites that block or slow-load in a bare Chromium context — a hard 15-second timeout reports that as a failure rather than hanging. The captured image is in-memory only, same as DEC-076's prospect selection: never written to evidence storage, never treated as scored or claimed fact. It is explicitly labelled on screen as a reference aid, not evidence — if demonstration-building is ever wired to reuse a captured screenshot as demonstration imagery, that would need its own decision under DEC-025's real-photos-or-labelled-placeholders rule, since a live screenshot of the candidate's own site is arguably neither. 6 new tests (`website-screenshot.test.ts`, fake window factory covering capture, https/hostname rejection, timeout, and load failure, each confirming the window is always destroyed). Suite reaches 163.
- Supersedes: not applicable

### DEC-079 — A real demonstration-site generator: pure, unpublished, honest about gaps

- Date: 2026-08-08
- Status: **accepted, operator-directed, 2026-08-08** ("Generador de HTML primero" — the operator's own choice between building the HTML generator now versus researching Cloudflare publish automation first; also confirmed adding a `phone` field to discovery candidates before generation, since charter 15.2/DEC-023 require a click-to-call link). Verified in the isolated sandbox (lint, both `tsc` configs, `vitest run` at 170/170, `vite build`, all clean). Not yet exercised live.
